126. The processes that bring about involution of the uterus are:-
A. Autolysis , hypertrophy
B. Hemolysis , ischemia
C. Hypertrophy , hemolysis
D. Ischemia , autolysis
Answer: D. Ischemia , autolysis
Rationale: Uterine involution occurs through ischaemia (reduced blood supply as the muscle fibres contract and retract) and autolysis (enzymatic self-digestion of excess muscle protein), together with phagocytosis. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives. Hypertrophy and haemolysis are not involution processes.
127. Rubella’s eye manifestation on the newborn is mainly:-
A. Cataract
B. Microcephaly
C. Retinopathy
D. Ophthalmia Neonatorum
Answer: A. Cataract
Rationale: Congenital rubella syndrome classically causes congenital cataract as the main ocular manifestation, alongside cardiac defects and sensorineural deafness. Reference: Kenya paediatric/newborn guidelines; Nelson Paediatrics. Microcephaly is neurological, and ophthalmia neonatorum is a gonococcal/chlamydial conjunctivitis, not rubella.
128. Infants with necrotizing enterocolitis presents with: -
A. Delayed passage of meconium, abdomen distension
B. Abdomen distension, bile aspirate
C. Bile stained vomitus, delayed passage of meconium
D. Abdomen distension, gangrene of distal rectum
Answer: B. Abdomen distension, bile aspirate
Rationale: Necrotizing enterocolitis in the neonate presents with abdominal distension, feeding intolerance with bilious (bile-stained) gastric aspirate, and later bloody stools (Kenya newborn care/Nelson Paediatrics). Delayed passage of meconium is characteristic of Hirschsprung disease/intestinal obstruction rather than NEC. Option B (abdominal distension plus bile aspirate) best captures the classic NEC presentation.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
129. A firm cushion of adipose tissue over the symphysis pubis covered by curly hair is:-
A. Labia majora
B. Mons pubis
C. Fourchette
D. Hymen
Answer: B. Mons pubis
Rationale: The mons pubis (mons veneris) is a firm cushion of adipose (fatty) tissue lying over the symphysis pubis, covered with curly pubic hair after puberty. This is standard female external genital anatomy per Myles Textbook for Midwives.
130. Sign of cardiac compromise in pregnancy is:-
A. Left axis deviation
B. Systolic murmur
C. Arrhythmia
D. High pressure
Answer: C. Arrhythmia
Rationale: In pregnancy a systolic (flow) murmur and left axis deviation are normal physiological findings due to the hyperdynamic circulation and diaphragmatic elevation. An arrhythmia (irregular rhythm) is pathological and signals cardiac compromise/decompensation per Myles and Kenya maternal guidelines.
131. The effects of pregnancy on diabetes are:-
A. Increased insulin antagonists, glycosuria
B. Polyhydramnios, obstructed labour
C. Glycosylated haemoglobin, obstructed labour
D. Placenta abruption, glycosuria
Answer: A. Increased insulin antagonists, glycosuria
Rationale: Pregnancy is diabetogenic: placental hormones (hPL, cortisol, oestrogen, progesterone) act as insulin antagonists increasing insulin resistance, and the lowered renal threshold produces glycosuria. Polyhydramnios and placental abruption are effects of diabetes on pregnancy, not the reverse.
132. Features of true labor include:-
A. Regular uterine contractions, Cervix dilatation, intensity remains the same
B. Intensity increases, bulging of membranes, contractions irregular
C. Bulging membranes, dilatation and effacement present, pain well relieved
D. Bulging membranes, descent of presenting part progressive, regular contractions
Answer: A. Regular uterine contractions, Cervix dilatation, intensity remains the same
Rationale: True labour is characterised by regular, progressively strengthening uterine contractions accompanied by cervical effacement and dilatation. Although true contractions actually increase in intensity, this is the only option combining regular contractions with cervical dilatation (the defining feature); the other options describe false labour features such as irregular contractions and pain relieved by rest.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
133. In prenatal infection:-
A. Cytomegalovirus causes macrosomic babies
B. Rubella can be prevented by administration of rubella vaccine uring pregnancy
C. Toxoplasma is a virus
D. HIV virus infect the baby more readily when delivered vaginally than caesarean section
Answer: D. HIV virus infect the baby more readily when delivered vaginally than caesarean section
Rationale: Vaginal delivery carries higher vertical HIV transmission risk than elective caesarean due to intrapartum blood/secretion exposure. Rubella vaccine is contraindicated in pregnancy (live vaccine), CMV causes microcephaly/IUGR not macrosomia, and toxoplasma is a protozoan parasite, not a virus.
134. Normal findings on a healthy newborn include:-
A. Heart rate 120-160, Respiratory rate 40-60bpm, Temperature 36.1-37 o C
B. Heart rate100-120, respiratory rate 30-60, weight 2.5-3.5kgs
C. Respiratory rate 20-40, Heart rate 80-120, Temperature 36.5-37.5 o C
D. Respiratory Rate below 40, Heart rate 120-140bpm, Temperature 36.5-37.5 o C
Answer: A. Heart rate 120-160, Respiratory rate 40-60bpm, Temperature 36.1-37 o C
Rationale: Normal newborn vital signs are heart rate 120-160 bpm, respiratory rate 40-60 breaths/min, and axillary temperature within the normal range. This is the only option pairing the correct HR and RR ranges per Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols and newborn care guidelines.
135. Physiological changes in the reproductive system include:-
A. There is no changes in the vagina
B. The uterus first enlarge by hyperplasia then by hypertrophy
C. There is no change in the cervix
D. Estrogen has no role in any changes during pregnancy
Answer: B. The uterus first enlarge by hyperplasia then by hypertrophy
Rationale: Uterine growth in pregnancy occurs initially by hyperplasia (increase in cell number) under oestrogen influence in early pregnancy, then predominantly by hypertrophy (enlargement of existing myometrial cells) as pregnancy advances. The vagina and cervix also undergo marked changes and oestrogen is central, making the other options false.
136. The sutures of the foetal skull that meet at the bregma are:-
A. frontal, 2 parietal, 1 labdoidal
B. 2 frontal, 1 labdoidal, 1 occipital
C. 2 sagittal, 2 coronal, 1 frontal
D. 1 frontal, 2 coronal 1 sagittal
Answer: D. 1 frontal, 2 coronal 1 sagittal
Rationale: The bregma (anterior fontanelle) is where four sutures meet: the single frontal (metopic) suture anteriorly, the two coronal sutures laterally, and the sagittal suture posteriorly. This is the classic description in Myles Textbook for Midwives.
137. Hyperextension of the fetal head is found in:-
A. Vertex presentation.
B. Face presentation.
C. Shoulder presentation.
D. Breech presentation
Answer: B. Face presentation.
Rationale: Face presentation results from complete hyperextension (deflexion) of the fetal head so that the occiput lies against the fetal back and the face presents. Vertex presentation is well flexed, whereas brow is partial extension.
138. During the first 48 hours of puerperium:-
A. The cervix is soft and flabby
B. The uterus weighs approximately 60 g
C. Diameter of placental site measures 7.5 cm
D. Uterine fundus is palpable 5 cm above the umbilicus
Answer: A. The cervix is soft and flabby
Rationale: In the first 24-48 hours of the puerperium the cervix remains soft, flabby and easily distensible before gradually regaining tone. The uterus weighs about 1000 g (not 60 g) immediately after birth, the placental site is larger initially, and the fundus lies at/near the umbilicus, not 5 cm above it, per Myles.
139. The WHO key factor in warm chain in newborn care is:-
A. Minimize neonatal exposure to sclera Neonatorum
B. Minimize risk of exposure to neonatal hypothermia
C. Reduce nosocomial infections
D. Elicit bonding
Answer: B. Minimize risk of exposure to neonatal hypothermia
Rationale: The WHO 'warm chain' is a set of interlinked procedures at birth and during the first days of life that minimize the newborn's risk of hypothermia and ensure thermal protection. Reference: WHO Thermal Protection of the Newborn; Kenya Newborn Care guidelines.
140. The midwife knows a fetus is well oxygenated during: -
A. Peak of uterine contractions
B. Increment of uterine contraction
C. Decrement of uterine contraction
D. Relaxation between contractions
Answer: D. Relaxation between contractions
Rationale: During uterine relaxation between contractions, uteroplacental blood flow is restored, allowing optimal fetal oxygenation. At the peak of contractions placental perfusion is reduced. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya EmONC.
141. The normal color of the stool of a newborn on the 3rd -5th day is:-
A. Pale
B. Blackish green
C. Yellow
D. Brownish yellow
Answer: C. Yellow
Rationale: By day 3-5, after meconium (blackish-green) has passed and transitional stools clear, the breastfed newborn passes soft yellow stools. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya Newborn Care.
142. Signs and symptoms of pulmonary embolism include:-
A. Chest pain, dyspnoea, cough.
B. Hypertension, dyspnoea, chest pain.
C. Hypertension, pyrexia, tachycardia
D. Hypertension, pyrexia, cough.
Answer: A. Chest pain, dyspnoea, cough.
Rationale: Pulmonary embolism classically presents with sudden dyspnoea, pleuritic chest pain and cough (sometimes haemoptysis), with tachycardia and hypotension rather than hypertension. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
143. Fetal tachycardia could result from:-
A. Labetalol ingestion
B. Post maturity
C. Maternal febrile illness
D. Maternal hypothyroidism
Answer: C. Maternal febrile illness
Rationale: Maternal fever/infection raises fetal metabolic rate and produces fetal tachycardia. Beta-blockers (labetalol), post-maturity and maternal hypothyroidism are associated with fetal bradycardia rather than tachycardia. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives.
144. An absolute indication for an elective caeserian-section is :-
A. Previous CS.
B. Antepartum hemorrhage.
C. Placenta previa totalis.
D. Fetal distress.
Answer: C. Placenta previa totalis.
Rationale: A complete/major placenta praevia covering the internal os is an absolute indication for elective caesarean section as vaginal delivery is impossible. The other options are relative or emergency indications. Reference: Kenya EmONC; Myles Textbook for Midwives.
145. In ectopic pregnancy: -
A. IM progesterone is useful
B. 50% continued up to term
C. Rarely diagnosed before 12 weeks of gestation
D. Usually present as obstetric emergency before 12 weeks gestation
Answer: D. Usually present as obstetric emergency before 12 weeks gestation
Rationale: Ectopic pregnancy typically presents as an acute obstetric emergency (rupture, pain, shock) in the first trimester, usually before 12 weeks. It cannot continue to term and progesterone is not therapeutic. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya EmONC.
146. Regarding endometrial changes during the menstrual cycle:-
A. The basal layer of the endometrium is responsive to hormonal stimulation.
B. The functional layer of the endometrium remains intact throughout the menstrual cycle.
C. The increased thickness of the endometrium during the proliferative phase is due to estrogen action.
D. Estrogen induces secretory changes in the endometrium & reduces mitotic activity.
Answer: C. The increased thickness of the endometrium during the proliferative phase is due to estrogen action.
Rationale: During the proliferative phase, rising estrogen from the developing follicle stimulates regeneration and thickening of the endometrial functional layer. The functional layer is shed at menstruation and progesterone (not estrogen) induces secretory changes. Reference: Guyton/Anatomy & Physiology; Myles.
147. Antenatal mothers who are sero-reactive and were not previously on HAART should be started on antiretroviral therapy as early as:-
A. 28 weeks of gestation.
B. 16 weeks of gestation.
C. 36 weeks of gestation.
D. 14 weeks of gestation.
Answer: D. 14 weeks of gestation.
Rationale: Under Kenya PMTCT/Option B+ guidelines, ART is started for life in all HIV-positive pregnant women as early as possible; the earliest recommended time in ANC after the first trimester is around 14 weeks. Reference: Kenya ARV/PMTCT Guidelines.
148. Engagement is strictly defined as:-
A. When the presenting part goes through the pelvic inlet.
B. When the presenting part is level with the ischial spines.
C. When the greatest Biparietal diameter of the fetal head passes the pelvic inlet.
D. When the greatest Biparietal diameter of the fetal head is at the level of ischial spines
Answer: C. When the greatest Biparietal diameter of the fetal head passes the pelvic inlet.
Rationale: Engagement is strictly defined as the passage of the widest transverse diameter of the presenting part (the biparietal diameter in a cephalic presentation) through the pelvic brim/inlet. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives.
149. The use of progesterone only pills: -
A. Ovulation is not regularly inhibited
B. Protection against is as good as combined pill
C. There is a risk of DVT
D. Breakthrough bleeding is rare
Answer: A. Ovulation is not regularly inhibited
Rationale: Progesterone-only pills (POPs) work mainly by thickening cervical mucus and thinning the endometrium; ovulation is inhibited only inconsistently (in about 40-60% of cycles). They carry no significant DVT risk (unlike combined pills), are slightly less effective than the combined pill, and breakthrough/irregular bleeding is common, not rare. Kenya National Family Planning Guidelines; WHO MEC.
150. Lightening is defined as:-
A. Formation of the lower uterine segment
B. Descent of the fetus into the lower uterine pole
C. Neuromuscular harmony of the uterus
D. Division of the uterus into upper and lower uterine segments
Answer: B. Descent of the fetus into the lower uterine pole
Rationale: Lightening is the descent of the presenting part into the pelvis/lower uterine pole in the weeks before labour, relieving pressure on the diaphragm as the uterus sinks. This is distinct from formation or division of uterine segments. Myles Textbook for Midwives.
151. Fundal contraction force transmitted to upper pole of the uterus down along the axis of foetus and applied by the presenting part to the cervix is:-
A. General fluid pressure
B. Retraction pressure
C. Foetal axis pressure
D. Fundal dominance
Answer: C. Foetal axis pressure
Rationale: Fetal axis pressure is the force of the fundal contraction transmitted from the upper pole down the long axis of the fetus, so the presenting part is applied to the cervix, aiding dilatation. General fluid pressure acts via the forewaters; fundal dominance describes the wave pattern. Myles Textbook for Midwives.
152. By the end of pueperium, the size of the uterus measures:-
A. 15 x 11 x 7.5 cm
B. 30 x 22 x 20 cm
C. 7.5 x 5 x 2.5 cm
D. 15 ×22× 7.5 cm
Answer: C. 7.5 x 5 x 2.5 cm
Rationale: By the end of the puerperium involution returns the uterus to its non-pregnant dimensions of approximately 7.5 x 5 x 2.5 cm, weighing around 60 g. The larger dimensions describe the term or immediate postpartum uterus. Myles Textbook for Midwives.
153. During vacuum extraction the cup is applied:-
A. 1-2 cm posterior to the anterior fontanelle
B. 2-3 cm anterior to posterior fontanelle
C. At the center of coronal suture
D. On the posterior fontanelle
Answer: A. 1-2 cm posterior to the anterior fontanelle
Rationale: For vacuum extraction the cup is centred over the flexion point, about 3 cm anterior to the posterior fontanelle (equivalently 1-2 cm posterior to the anterior fontanelle) along the sagittal suture, promoting flexion of the head. Kenya EmONC guidelines; Myles.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
154. The anterior posterior diameter of the outlet measures from the:-
A. Iliopectineal eminence to sacro iliac joint
B. Sacroiliac joint to iliopectinelal eminence
C. Sacrospinous ligaments to obturator foramen
D. Symphysis pubis to sacrococcygeal ligaments
Answer: D. Symphysis pubis to sacrococcygeal ligaments
Rationale: The anteroposterior diameter of the pelvic outlet runs from the lower border of the symphysis pubis to the sacrococcygeal junction, measuring about 13 cm. The other options describe brim/oblique landmarks. Myles Textbook for Midwives.
155. A patient in the third trimester of pregnancy is seen in the emergency room and while being examined has a convulsion. The doctor should immediately: -
A. Obtain neurologic consultation
B. Obtain psychiatric consultation
C. Give IV valium
D. Protect the patient from self-harm
Answer: D. Protect the patient from self-harm
Rationale: A convulsion in the third trimester indicates eclampsia; the immediate priority is patient safety—protecting her from injury and maintaining a patent airway during the fit. Magnesium sulphate (not diazepam) is the anticonvulsant of choice, given after the airway/safety are secured. Kenya EmONC/Maternal guidelines.
156. Fetal circulation which carries deoxygenated blood and wastes products from the fetus to the placenta for excretion are:-
A. Umbilical vein
B. Foramen ovale
C. Ductus arteriosus
D. Umbilical arteries
Answer: D. Umbilical arteries
Rationale: The two umbilical arteries carry deoxygenated blood and waste products from the fetus to the placenta for exchange/excretion. The umbilical vein carries oxygenated blood to the fetus; the foramen ovale and ductus arteriosus are intracardiac/great-vessel shunts. Myles Textbook for Midwives.
157. Indirect cause of maternal death in Kenya is:-
A. Obstructed labour
B. Anemia
C. Sepsis
D. Unsafe abortions
Answer: B. Anemia
Rationale: Indirect maternal deaths result from pre-existing or non-obstetric conditions aggravated by pregnancy, such as anaemia. Obstructed labour, sepsis and unsafe abortion are direct obstetric causes. Kenya Confidential Enquiry into Maternal Deaths; MOH.
158. A patient sustained a laceration of the premium during delivery, it involved the muscles of Perineal body but not the anal sphincter. Such a laceration would be classified as :-
A. First degree
B. Second degree
C. Third degree
D. Forth degree
Answer: B. Second degree
Rationale: A second-degree perineal tear involves the perineal skin, vaginal mucosa and the muscles of the perineal body but does not reach the anal sphincter. Third degree involves the anal sphincter and fourth degree extends into the rectal mucosa. Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya EmONC.
159. Acute uterine inversion following childbirth may be managed by:-
A. Giving the woman a bolus of 20 mls of 50% dextrose 2 hourly for eight hours
B. Attempting to restore the uterus along the direction of the vagina towards the posterior fornix
C. Removing the placenta from uterine wall if still adherent then restoring the uterus by hydrostatic pressure
D. Giving intramyometrial syntocinon then removing the placenta if still attached to the uterus
Answer: C. Removing the placenta from uterine wall if still adherent then restoring the uterus by hydrostatic pressure
Rationale: In acute uterine inversion, the classic management includes replacement of the uterus; where the placenta is still adherent it should not be removed before replacement in the immediate manual approach, but the hydrostatic (O'Sullivan) method involves reduction using warm-saline hydrostatic pressure. Among the options, C best describes an accepted approach (removal of adherent placenta then hydrostatic replacement). Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya EmONC guidelines.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
160. Sickle cell: -
A. Diseases presents in a heterotype (SA).
B. Increases the risk of urinary tract infections.
C. Iron binding capacity
D. Should be always given iron to avoid anemia.
Answer: B. Increases the risk of urinary tract infections.
Rationale: Sickle cell disease impairs renal medullary concentrating ability and predisposes to urinary stasis and infection, increasing UTI risk (especially in pregnancy). Heterozygous state is sickle cell trait not disease, iron is contraindicated unless deficient, so those options are wrong. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya haematology guidelines.
161. Bacterial Vaginosis: -
A. Is rare vaginal infection.
B. Is always symptomatic.
C. Is usually associated with profound inflammatory reaction.
D. Causes fishy discharge which results from bacterial amine production.
Answer: D. Causes fishy discharge which results from bacterial amine production.
Rationale: Bacterial vaginosis produces a thin greyish-white discharge with a fishy odour from amines (putrescine, cadaverine) released by anaerobic bacteria, positive on whiff test. It is common, often asymptomatic, and characteristically lacks inflammation (hence 'vaginosis' not 'vaginitis'). Reference: Kenya STI guidelines; Brunner & Suddarth.
162. At term, the ligaments of the pelvis change. This can result in:-
A. Increasing rigidity of the pelvis.
B. Degeneration of pelvic ground substance.
C. Decreasing width of the symphysis.
D. Enlargement of the pelvic cavity.
Answer: D. Enlargement of the pelvic cavity.
Rationale: Under the influence of relaxin and progesterone the pelvic ligaments and symphysis pubis soften and relax at term, permitting slight increase in joint mobility and effective enlargement of the pelvic cavity to aid delivery. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives.
163. The most common cause of ectopic pregnancy is:-
A. History of pelvic inflammatory disease
B. Congenital anomalies of the tube
C. Endometriosis
D. Tubal surgery
Answer: A. History of pelvic inflammatory disease
Rationale: The commonest cause/risk factor for ectopic pregnancy is prior PID (often chlamydial/gonococcal) causing tubal scarring and impaired ovum transport. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya reproductive health guidelines.
164. A bishop’s score of 4 indicates that:-
A. Labour is progressing well and augmentation yields good results.
B. There is poor prognosis therefore do not augment.
C. Amniotomy can be done with or without oxytocics.
D. There is possibility of good prognosis therefore augment.
Answer: D. There is possibility of good prognosis therefore augment.
Rationale: A Bishop score of 4 (favourable trend, generally a score above 3-5 considered increasingly favourable) suggests a reasonable prognosis for successful induction/augmentation. Of the options, D best fits that a score of 4 indicates possibility of good prognosis warranting augmentation. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya labour management guidelines.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
165. The coccyx articulates with the fifth sacral vertebrae to form: -
A. Sacrospinous joint
B. Sacroiliac joint
C. Symphysis pubis
D. Sacrococcygeal joint
Answer: D. Sacrococcygeal joint
Rationale: The coccyx articulates with the fifth (last) sacral vertebra to form the sacrococcygeal joint, a secondary cartilaginous joint that allows backward movement during delivery. Reference: Anatomy; Myles Textbook for Midwives.
166. Conception is most likely to occur when:-
A. Estrogen levels are low
B. Luteinizing hormone is high
C. Endometrial lining is thin
D. Progesterone level is low
Answer: B. Luteinizing hormone is high
Rationale: Conception is most likely around ovulation, which is triggered by the mid-cycle LH surge (high LH), following peak estrogen and with a thick receptive endometrium. Reference: Physiology of the menstrual cycle; Myles Textbook for Midwives.
167. A higher blood volume is required in pregnancy to:-
A. Maintain normal vascular peripheral resistance
B. Increase flow to the liver for adequate metabolism
C. Counter balance effects of lowered osmotic pressure and immunity
D. Counter balance the effects of increased arterial and venous capacity
Answer: D. Counter balance the effects of increased arterial and venous capacity
Rationale: Blood volume rises about 40-50% in pregnancy to fill the expanded vascular bed (increased arterial and venous capacity from vasodilatation and the added uteroplacental circulation) and to safeguard against blood loss at delivery. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives.
168. You are asked to see a 21-year-old woman for preconception care. She was diagnosed with generalised tonic-clonic epilepsy four years ago. This is poorly controlled. She is currently on sodium valproate and levetiracetam. What is the next step in her management?
A. Arrange MRI
B. Review medication
C. Commence aspirin 75 mg
D. Commence folic acid 5 mg
Answer: B. Review medication
Rationale: Sodium valproate is highly teratogenic (neural tube and other defects, neurodevelopmental harm) and must be avoided in women of childbearing potential; with poorly controlled epilepsy the priority preconception step is to review/switch antiepileptic medication to a safer agent under specialist care before pregnancy. High-dose folic acid 5 mg is also needed but medication review is the essential next step given valproate. Reference: Kenya/NCK pharmacology and preconception guidelines.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
169. Pawlick’s maneuver:-
A. Usually done during lateral palpation of the abdomen
B. Helps diagnose the lie of the fetus in the uterus
C. Helps determine the size, flexion and motility of the fetal head
D. Use in the second stage of labour ward to determine the position of the membranes.
Answer: C. Helps determine the size, flexion and motility of the fetal head
Rationale: Pawlik's manoeuvre is a deep pelvic grip in which the midwife grasps the lower pole of the uterus between thumb and fingers over the symphysis pubis to assess the presenting part (usually the head), determining its size, degree of flexion and mobility/engagement. It is not the lateral grip and does not diagnose lie. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; NCK antenatal abdominal examination.
170. During the 2nd stage of labor, external rotation of the fetal head assists the midwife to realize that: -
A. Foetal shoulders have descended
B. Foetal body is in the lateral position
C. Foetal shoulders have rotated into anterior-posterior of the outlet
D. Occiput has slipped under the sub-pubic
Answer: A. Foetal shoulders have descended
Rationale: After the head delivers and restitutes, external rotation follows because the shoulders rotate to bring the bisacromial diameter into the antero-posterior of the outlet as they descend; the visible external rotation of the head therefore signals that the shoulders have descended and rotated internally. This alerts the midwife to prepare for delivery of the shoulders. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives, mechanism of normal labour.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
171. The prophylactic measures PMTCT among HIV positive mothers is:-
A. AZT should be started as early as 14 weeks gestation
B. TDF + 3TC + DTG
C. AZT +3TC + DTG for 6 weeks
D. Single dose NVP should be given for 7 days post-partum
Answer: B. TDF + 3TC + DTG
Rationale: Current Kenya MOH/ARV PMTCT guidelines recommend lifelong triple ART (Option B+) for all HIV-positive pregnant women, with the first-line regimen being TDF + 3TC + DTG started immediately regardless of gestation or CD4. Single-dose NVP and short AZT-only courses are outdated. Reference: Kenya ARV Guidelines / PMTCT.
172. The maternal surface of the placenta contains: -
A. Chorion and visible blood vessels
B. Cotyledons and amnion
C. Blood vessel and amnion
D. Cotyledons and chorion
Answer: D. Cotyledons and chorion
Rationale: The maternal (uterine) surface of the placenta is dark red, rough and divided into 15-20 lobes called cotyledons, and is covered by a thin layer of the chorion (basal decidua/chorionic tissue). The fetal surface is the smooth, shiny one covered by amnion with visible vessels. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives, placenta at term.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
173. The best time to infiltrate the perineum with lignocaine is:-
A. Before performing an episiotomy
B. After performing an episiotomy
C. After delivery of the baby
D. Before suturing an episiotomy
Answer: A. Before performing an episiotomy
Rationale: Lignocaine should be infiltrated into the perineum before performing an episiotomy so that the incision is made while the tissue is anaesthetised, providing pain relief for the cut and later repair. Infiltrating after the cut defeats the analgesic purpose. Reference: Kenya EmONC / Myles Textbook for Midwives, episiotomy procedure.
174. Kjelland's forceps: -
A. May be used if the head is not engaged
B. Have no cephalic curve
C. Have knobs on the shank which point towards the sinciput
D. Have a sliding lock in the order to correct asynclitism
Answer: D. Have a sliding lock in the order to correct asynclitism
Rationale: Kjelland's forceps have a sliding lock which allows correction of asynclitism, minimal pelvic curve, and are used for rotational (e.g. occipito-posterior/transverse) deliveries with the head engaged. The knobs on the shank point toward the occiput, not sinciput. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives, instrumental delivery.
175. The most common complication of breast feeding is:-
A. Amenorrhea.
B. Excessive weight loss.
C. Puerperal mastitis.
D. Breast abscess.
Answer: C. Puerperal mastitis.
Rationale: Puerperal mastitis is the most common complication associated with breastfeeding, arising from milk stasis and cracked nipples allowing bacterial entry; if untreated it may progress to a breast abscess (a less common sequel). Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives; Kenya postnatal care guidelines.
176. Foetal complications resulting from obstructed labour include:-
A. Meconium aspiration, chorioamnionitis
B. Intra- uterine hypoxia, neonatal jaundice
C. Neonatal jaundice, meconium aspiration
D. Intrauterine hypoxia, ascending infection
Answer: D. Intrauterine hypoxia, ascending infection
Rationale: Obstructed labour causes fetal intra-uterine hypoxia/asphyxia from impaired placental perfusion and, with prolonged rupture of membranes, ascending intra-uterine infection (chorioamnionitis/sepsis). These are the direct fetal complications. Reference: Kenya EmONC; Myles Textbook for Midwives.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
177. The Chorion originates from:-
A. Inner cell mass.
B. Trophoblast.
C. Human Chorionic gonadotrophin.
D. Amniotic fluid
Answer: B. Trophoblast.
Rationale: The chorion develops from the trophoblast (outer cell layer of the blastocyst) together with extra-embryonic mesoderm, forming the outer fetal membrane and later the fetal part of the placenta. The inner cell mass forms the embryo and amnion. Reference: Myles Textbook for Midwives, embryology.
178. Kangaroo technique to ensure warmth to a low-birth-weight baby involves:-
A. Dry the baby properly and wrap with warm clothes, covering head with hat
B. Room temperature of 21-24 0 C and baby dressed in warm clothing
C. Baby placed inside the blouse to allow skin to skin contact and baby breastfed freely
D. Mother sleeps in semi-sitting position and covers herself well to keep the baby warm
Answer: C. Baby placed inside the blouse to allow skin to skin contact and baby breastfed freely
Rationale: Kangaroo Mother Care (KMC) is defined by continuous skin-to-skin contact between the mother's chest and the naked baby, held inside the mother's clothing, together with exclusive/frequent breastfeeding. This maintains the LBW baby's warmth and promotes bonding per Kenya Basic Newborn/KMC guidelines and Myles. The other options describe general thermal care but not the defining KMC technique.
179. The volume of amniotic fluid is:-
A. Maybe predicted by ultrasound
B. Is reduced in sever rhesus disease
C. Increases following amniocentesis
D. Is closely related to the fetal crown-rump length in the 3rd trimester of pregnancy
Answer: A. Maybe predicted by ultrasound
Rationale: Amniotic fluid volume can be estimated/predicted by ultrasound (amniotic fluid index / deepest pool). It is INCREASED (not reduced) in severe rhesus disease due to fetal hydrops/polyuria, is not increased by amniocentesis, and is not closely related to crown-rump length (a first-trimester measure). Per Myles Textbook for Midwives, ultrasound assessment is the correct statement.
180. Woods’ manoeuvre is applied when:-
A. Exert pressure on the posterior shoulder to bring posterior shoulder forward
B. Baby head is returned into vagina and taken in for caesarian section
C. Pressure applied at the back of fetal shoulder towards the fetal chest just above the symphsis pubis.
D. Assisting the mother onto her hands and knees
Answer: A. Exert pressure on the posterior shoulder to bring posterior shoulder forward
Rationale: Woods' (corkscrew) manoeuvre for shoulder dystocia involves inserting a hand vaginally and applying pressure on the anterior aspect of the posterior shoulder to rotate it, bringing the posterior shoulder forward around the pelvis to disimpact the anterior shoulder. Option C describes suprapubic pressure, and D describes the all-fours (Gaskin) manoeuvre. Per Myles/EmONC guidelines this rotational internal manoeuvre is Woods'.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
181. Upon examination of a pregnant woman at 20 weeks’ gestation, the nurse notes that the vaginal and cervix is deep reddish purple color. The nurse would record this as:-
A. Hegars sign
B. Goodels sign
C. Chadwicks sign
D. Hyperpigmentation of the genitalia
Answer: C. Chadwicks sign
Rationale: Chadwick's sign is the bluish/deep reddish-purple (violet) discolouration of the vagina and cervix due to increased vascularity in pregnancy. Hegar's sign is softening of the lower uterine segment/isthmus, and Goodell's sign is softening of the cervix. This is a classic probable sign of pregnancy per Kozier/Myles.
182. In Schultz mechanism during third stage of labour:-
A. The placenta begins to separate from one of the lateral borders
B. Blood from placental site escapes so that separation is unaided by a retro placental clot
C. The placenta descends, slipping sideways, maternal surface first at the vulva
D. Placenta begins to separate centrally and a retro placental clot is formed aiding the separation
Answer: D. Placenta begins to separate centrally and a retro placental clot is formed aiding the separation
Rationale: In the Schultze mechanism of placental separation, the placenta separates centrally first, a retroplacental clot forms behind it aiding separation, and it descends fetal (shiny) surface first. Options describing lateral separation and maternal surface presenting first describe the Matthews Duncan mechanism. Per Myles Textbook for Midwives.
183. Major factor for the fetal RBC to cross to the mother:-
A. Labor and delivery
B. Spontaneous abortion
C. Low Abruption placenta
D. Placenta praevia
Answer: A. Labor and delivery
Rationale: The major cause of fetomaternal haemorrhage (fetal RBCs crossing into maternal circulation, causing Rh sensitisation) is labour and delivery, when placental separation allows the greatest transfer of fetal cells. While abortion, abruption and other events can cause some transfer, delivery is the principal/major sensitising event. Per Myles and Kenya EmONC/Rh prophylaxis guidance.
184. Lateral palpation of the abdomen in a gravid woman determines:-
A. Fungal height
B. Fetal presentation
C. Position of the fetus
D. Engagement of fetus
Answer: C. Position of the fetus
Rationale: Lateral palpation of the abdomen locates the fetal back and limbs on either side of the maternal abdomen, thereby determining the position (and lie) of the fetus. Fundal height/fundal palpation assesses the pole in the fundus, pelvic palpation assesses presentation and engagement. Per Myles antenatal abdominal examination technique.
185. The treatment choice for gardenerella vaginosis is :-
A. Ampicillin.
B. Metronidazole.
C. Clindamycin.
D. Gentamycin.
Answer: B. Metronidazole.
Rationale: Bacterial vaginosis (Gardnerella vaginalis) is treated first-line with oral metronidazole per Kenya STI/reproductive health guidelines. Clindamycin is an alternative, but metronidazole is the treatment of choice. Ampicillin and gentamicin are not indicated.
Year: 2026
186. The disadvantages of perineal prostatectomy include: -
A. Retrograde ejaculation, urethral trauma and stricture development.
B. Haemorrhage that is difficult to control, prolonged recovery and discomfort.
C. Requires highly trained surgeon, impotence and seizures.
D. Higher post-operative incidence of impotence, greater potential for infection.
Answer: D. Higher post-operative incidence of impotence, greater potential for infection.
Rationale: The perineal approach to prostatectomy is associated with a higher incidence of impotence, incontinence, and rectal injury, and because the incision is near the rectum there is greater potential for wound infection (Brunner & Suddarth). This directly matches option D.
187. The main goal of treatment for acute glomerulonephritis is: -
A. Encourage activity.
B. Encourage high protein diet.
C. Maintain fluid balance.
D. Teach intermittent urinary catheterization.
Answer: C. Maintain fluid balance.
Rationale: In acute glomerulonephritis the kidneys retain sodium and water causing oedema and hypertension, so the main treatment goal is to maintain fluid and electrolyte balance while preserving renal function (Brunner & Suddarth). Protein is restricted, not increased, and activity is limited during the acute phase.
188. The three primary symptoms of Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) are: -
A. Chronic cough, sputum production and dyspnea on exertion
B. Barrel chest, clubbed fingers and dyspnea on exertion
C. Peripheral oedema, dyspnea, chronic coughing
D. Rhinitis, wheezing and chronic cough
Answer: A. Chronic cough, sputum production and dyspnea on exertion
Rationale: The three cardinal/primary symptoms of COPD are chronic cough, sputum (mucus) production, and dyspnoea on exertion (Brunner & Suddarth; GOLD). Barrel chest and clubbing are signs, not primary symptoms.
189. Maximum growth of head circumference is attained at age of: -
A. 1-3yrs
B. 4-5 yrs
C. 7-8 yrs
D. 10-15yrs
Answer: A. 1-3yrs
Rationale: Brain and head circumference growth is most rapid in early childhood; the maximum growth of head circumference occurs within the first 1-3 years, with the brain reaching most of its adult size by about age 3 (Kozier; paediatric growth references). Later years show minimal head circumference increase.
190. A client with pneumonia has a temperature of 39.5°C, pulse 120/min, respirations 32/min, and BP 110/70 mmHg. The vital sign change that is most directly related to hypoxemia is;
A. Elevated body temperature from infection process
B. Increased respiratory rate from impaired gas exchange
C. Stable blood pressure despite systemic inflammation
D. Mild tachycardia resulting from elevated temperature
Answer: B. Increased respiratory rate from impaired gas exchange
Rationale: Hypoxaemia stimulates chemoreceptors producing tachypnoea; the raised respiratory rate (32/min) reflects impaired gas exchange in pneumonia and is the sign most directly related to hypoxaemia (Brunner & Suddarth). Fever and tachycardia relate to infection/temperature rather than directly to hypoxaemia.
191. The priority indicator of increasing intracranial pressure is
A. Unequal pupil size
B. Decreasing systolic pressure
C. Tachycardia
D. Decreasing body temperature
Answer: A. Unequal pupil size
Rationale: Early and priority signs of increasing intracranial pressure include changes in level of consciousness and pupillary changes such as unequal (anisocoria) or sluggish pupils. The Cushing response of raised (not decreased) systolic pressure, bradycardia and altered respirations is a late sign (Brunner & Suddarth).
192. The typical clinical manifestations of acute pancreatitis include:-
A. Hypertension, epigastric pain, abdominal swelling
B. Acute epigastric pain, respiratory distress, hypotension
C. Dyspnea, hypertension, hypoglycemia
D. Hypervolemia, tachypnea, hyperglycemia
Answer: B. Acute epigastric pain, respiratory distress, hypotension
Rationale: Acute pancreatitis classically presents with severe acute epigastric pain radiating to the back, and in severe disease with respiratory distress and hypotension/shock from third-spacing of fluid (Brunner & Suddarth). Hyperglycaemia (not hypoglycaemia) may occur, making option B the best fit.
193. Indications for erythromycin include: -
A. Pneumonia, multiple sclerosis
B. Legionnaire’s disease, multiple sclerosis
C. Legionnaire’s disease, pneumonia
D. Multiple sclerosis, campylobacteria infection
Answer: C. Legionnaire’s disease, pneumonia
Rationale: Erythromycin, a macrolide, is indicated for respiratory infections including Legionnaire's disease and atypical/community-acquired pneumonia, as well as campylobacter infection (Kenya pharmacology guidelines; BNF). Multiple sclerosis is not an infection and is not an indication, eliminating the other options.
194. The diet implemented for a patient in acute renal failure is: -
A. High protein, high calorie, low sodium, low potassium
B. Low fat, low protein, high potassium, low sodium
C. High calorie, low protein, low sodium, low potassium
D. Bland, low calorie, low sodium, low potassium
Answer: C. High calorie, low protein, low sodium, low potassium
Rationale: In acute renal failure the diet provides high calories to prevent catabolism, with restriction of protein, sodium and potassium to reduce nitrogenous waste and prevent hyperkalaemia and fluid overload (Brunner & Suddarth). This matches option C.
195. In estimating the size of burns, the palm of the patient’s hand represents total body surface area of; -
A. 3%
B. 1%
C. 4.5%
D. 9%
Answer: B. 1%
Rationale: The 'rule of palm' estimates that the surface of the patient's palm (including fingers) represents approximately 1% of total body surface area, useful for estimating scattered burns (Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya burns guidelines).
196. The characteristics of a “Cushingoid” appearance in Cushing’s disease include:-
A. Flushing, brittle skin, abdominal pain and encephalopathy.
B. Hypotension, Kussmauls’ breathing, hunch back & diaphoresis
C. Moon face, buffalo hump, central obesity & thin musculature
D. Mood swings, slow wound healing, hypertension & tunnel vision
Answer: C. Moon face, buffalo hump, central obesity & thin musculature
Rationale: Cushing's disease results from excess cortisol, producing the classic Cushingoid habitus: moon (rounded) face, buffalo hump (dorsocervical fat pad), truncal/central obesity with thin wasted limb musculature due to protein catabolism. This is the standard description in Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
197. “Talk and die syndrome” is commonly associated with: -
A. Epidural hemorrhage.
B. Intracerebral bleeding.
C. Sub-arachnoid hemorrhage.
D. Cardiac Tamponade
Answer: A. Epidural hemorrhage.
Rationale: 'Talk and die' syndrome describes the classic lucid interval of an epidural (extradural) hematoma, where the patient is initially conscious/talking after head injury, then deteriorates as arterial bleeding (typically middle meningeal artery) compresses the brain. This lucid interval is characteristic of epidural hemorrhage per Brunner & Suddarth.
198. Factors that can lead to myocardial ischemia include;
A. Anemia, aortic valve stenosis, polycythemia
B. Mitral valve stenosis, anemia, left ventricular hypertrophy
C. Anemia, aortic valve stenosis, mitral valve stenosis
D. Polycythemia, mitral valve stenosis, left ventricular hypertrophy
Answer: A. Anemia, aortic valve stenosis, polycythemia
Rationale: Myocardial ischemia results from imbalance between oxygen supply and demand. Anemia reduces oxygen-carrying capacity, aortic stenosis increases myocardial workload/demand and reduces coronary perfusion, and polycythemia increases blood viscosity impairing coronary flow. All three provoke ischemia per Brunner & Suddarth cardiovascular pathophysiology.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
199. The condition in which the eyes balls are not properly aligned with each other is referred to as: -
A. Astigmatism
B. Strabismus
C. Presbyopia
D. Amblyopia
Answer: B. Strabismus
Rationale: Strabismus is the misalignment of the eyes (squint) where the eyeballs are not properly aligned with each other. Astigmatism is a refractive error, presbyopia is age-related loss of accommodation, and amblyopia is 'lazy eye' with reduced vision. Standard ophthalmology/Brunner definition.
200. After a clients’ nasogastric tube has been removed the nurse should,
A. Offer the client liquids to drink
B. Provide the client with oral hygiene
C. Encourage client to cough and deep breath
D. Auscultate the client’s bowel sounds
Answer: B. Provide the client with oral hygiene
Rationale: After nasogastric tube removal, the priority nursing action is to provide oral (and nasal) hygiene, as the tube causes drying, irritation and unpleasant taste. Oral intake is resumed only after confirming return of bowel function per orders. This is the standard post-NG-removal comfort measure in Kozier Fundamentals.
201. Swabs, instruments and sharps count during surgery is carried out by:-
A. Circulating and anaesthetic nurses
B. Anaesthetic and scrub nurses
C. Circulating and Scrub nurses
D. Recovery room and scrub nurses
Answer: C. Circulating and Scrub nurses
Rationale: The count of swabs, instruments and sharps is a shared responsibility performed together by the scrub (sterile) nurse and the circulating nurse, who verify and record counts before, during and after surgery to prevent retained items. This is the standard perioperative safety practice per Brunner & Suddarth.
202. Pre-operative nursing interventions include: -
A. Relieving pain, reducing anxiety, education and counselling.
B. Providing instruction, Care of prosthesis and maintaining fluid balance
C. Reducing anxiety, relieving discomfort and providing instruction.
D. Maintaining fluid balance, managing potential complications and relieving pain
Answer: C. Reducing anxiety, relieving discomfort and providing instruction.
Rationale: Pre-operative nursing interventions focus on reducing pre-op anxiety/fear, relieving existing discomfort, and providing pre-operative instruction/teaching (deep breathing, coughing, leg exercises). Options citing 'managing potential complications' and 'care of prosthesis' relate to post-operative care. This matches Brunner & Suddarth preoperative nursing management.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
203. In retinal detachment fluid accumulates between the: -
A. Sclera and ciliary body
B. Retina and choroid
C. Pupil and suspensory ligament
D. Lens and pupil
Answer: B. Retina and choroid
Rationale: In retinal detachment, the sensory (neural) retina separates from the underlying retinal pigment epithelium/choroid, with fluid accumulating in the potential space between the retina and choroid. This is the standard definition in Brunner & Suddarth ophthalmic nursing.
204. A client diagnosed with left-sided heart failure is most likely to exhibit:
A. Pulmonary crackles and exertional dyspnea symptoms
B. Bilateral calf pain and intermittent claudication
C. Ascites and hepatomegaly from venous congestion
D. Peripheral edema and jugular vein distension
Answer: A. Pulmonary crackles and exertional dyspnea symptoms
Rationale: Left-sided heart failure causes pulmonary venous congestion, producing pulmonary crackles, exertional dyspnea, orthopnea and cough. Peripheral edema, JVD, ascites and hepatomegaly are features of right-sided failure. This left-versus-right distinction is standard in Brunner & Suddarth.
205. A client arrives with suspected tension pneumothorax. The following assessment finding requires immediate intervention to the patient;
A. Productive cough with yellow sputum production
B. Diminished breath sounds on one side only
C. Mild chest discomfort during inspiration episodes
D. Respiratory rate of twenty-two breaths per minute
Answer: B. Diminished breath sounds on one side only
Rationale: In tension pneumothorax, unilaterally diminished/absent breath sounds indicate lung collapse and rising intrathoracic pressure requiring immediate needle decompression. The other findings (productive cough, mild discomfort, RR 22) are non-emergent. Recognizing absent unilateral breath sounds as the critical sign is per Brunner & Suddarth emergency care.
206. The priority interventions for a patient who has suffered a cardiac arrest are: -
A. High quality CPR and early defibrillation
B. Establishing IV access, history taking
C. Early defibrillation, administration of hydrocortisone
D. Taking a 12 lead ECG, administration of Atropine
Answer: A. High quality CPR and early defibrillation
Rationale: In cardiac arrest the priorities per Kenya EmONC/BLS-ACLS and AHA-aligned protocols are immediate high-quality chest compressions (CPR) and early defibrillation of shockable rhythms, which are the interventions most linked to survival. IV access, ECG and drugs are secondary and must not delay compressions/defibrillation.
207. A patient experiencing status epilepticus has been seizing continuously for 10 minutes. The medication that the nurse will administer first is;
A. Oral phenytoin
B. Oral carbamazepine
C. Intravenous lorazepam
D. Intramuscular vitamin K
Answer: C. Intravenous lorazepam
Rationale: Status epilepticus is first managed with a benzodiazepine; IV lorazepam is the first-line agent to abort ongoing seizure activity. Phenytoin and carbamazepine are not first-line abortive drugs (phenytoin is a second-line loading agent), and vitamin K is irrelevant. Per Kenya clinical guidelines and Brunner & Suddarth neurology.
208. The laboratory finding that is most consistent with iron-deficiency anemia is;
A. Elevated hemoglobin and increased ferritin levels
B. High reticulocyte count with polycythemia
C. Reduced hemoglobin and low serum ferritin
D. Increased hematocrit and elevated platelets
Answer: C. Reduced hemoglobin and low serum ferritin
Rationale: Iron-deficiency anaemia is characterised by low haemoglobin with low serum ferritin (depleted iron stores), a microcytic hypochromic picture. Elevated Hb, polycythaemia and raised haematocrit/platelets contradict iron deficiency. Per Brunner & Suddarth and Kenya haematology guidelines.
209. The main role played by a circulating nurse is to;
A. Intubating patient.
B. Extubating patient
C. Observing patient
D. Supporting the scrub nurse
Answer: D. Supporting the scrub nurse
Rationale: The circulating (unscrubbed) nurse works outside the sterile field, supporting the scrub nurse and surgical team by supplying instruments, positioning, documentation and coordinating the theatre. Intubation/extubation are anaesthetist roles. Per Kozier fundamentals and perioperative nursing standards.
210. In myocardial infarction, Nitroglycerin is administered to: -
A. Dilate arteries in small doses.
B. Decrease ischemia by reducing myocardial oxygen consumption.
C. Increase the preload.
D. Enable the patient achieve muscle relaxation.
Answer: B. Decrease ischemia by reducing myocardial oxygen consumption.
Rationale: Nitroglycerin is a venodilator that reduces preload (and afterload), thereby lowering myocardial oxygen demand and relieving ischaemic chest pain in MI; coronary vasodilation also improves supply. It does not increase preload. Per Brunner & Suddarth cardiac pharmacology.
211. Open angle glaucoma is mainly treated using,
A. Proton pump inhibitors, quinolones and glycosides
B. Antibiotics, osmotic diuretics and haematinics
C. Miotics, Carbonic anhydrase inhibitors and epinephrine
D. Stool softeners, anti-hypertensives and Miotics
Answer: C. Miotics, Carbonic anhydrase inhibitors and epinephrine
Rationale: Open-angle glaucoma is treated pharmacologically to lower intraocular pressure using miotics (e.g. pilocarpine), carbonic anhydrase inhibitors (e.g. acetazolamide) and adrenergic agents (epinephrine), plus beta-blockers/prostaglandins. The other options are unrelated drug classes. Per Brunner & Suddarth ophthalmology.
212. Barrel chest is a feature of;
A. OptionHeart failure, kyphosis
B. Kyphosis, emphysema
C. Emphysema, osteoarthritis
D. Drug resistant tuberculosis, emphysema
Answer: B. Kyphosis, emphysema
Rationale: Barrel chest (increased anteroposterior diameter) is a classic feature of emphysema/COPD due to hyperinflation, and is also associated with kyphosis of the thoracic spine. Osteoarthritis and heart failure do not cause barrel chest. Per Brunner & Suddarth respiratory assessment.
213. In diabetes management, self-monitoring of blood glucose is preferred to urine glucose monitoring because: -
A. It can be done by any client
B. It is not influenced by drugs
C. It is more accurate
D. It is easy to perform
Answer: C. It is more accurate
Rationale: Self-monitoring of blood glucose (SMBG) directly measures capillary blood glucose, giving a real-time accurate value, whereas urine glucose only becomes positive once the renal threshold (~10 mmol/L) is exceeded and reflects glucose over time, not the current level. Brunner & Suddarth and Kenya diabetes guidelines endorse SMBG for accurate glycaemic monitoring.
214. Sympathetic stimulation of the cardiovascular system causes: -
A. Increased heart rate and constriction of coronary arteries.
B. Constriction of GIT secretory gland vessels with increased flow of digestive juices.
C. Increased peripheral resistance and dilatation of coronary arteries.
D. Increased force of heart contraction & reduced peripheral resistance
Answer: C. Increased peripheral resistance and dilatation of coronary arteries.
Rationale: Sympathetic stimulation increases heart rate and contractility, causes vasoconstriction of most peripheral vessels (raising peripheral resistance), but dilates the coronary arteries to meet increased myocardial oxygen demand. Standard cardiovascular physiology (Guyton/anatomy & physiology texts).
215. The major electrolytes in the intracellular fluid are;
A. Sodium and chloride
B. Potassium and Chloride
C. Potassium and phosphorus
D. Sodium and phosphorus
Answer: C. Potassium and phosphorus
Rationale: The major intracellular cation is potassium and the major intracellular anion is phosphate (phosphorus), whereas sodium and chloride predominate extracellularly. Fluid and electrolyte physiology, Brunner & Suddarth.
216. The most relevant assessment for a patient admitted with benign prostatic hyperplasia is:
A. Perineal oedema
B. Urethral discharge
C. Flank pain radiating in the groin
D. Distension of the lower abdomen
Answer: D. Distension of the lower abdomen
Rationale: BPH obstructs the bladder outlet causing urinary retention, so the most relevant assessment is a distended lower abdomen (palpable full bladder) along with hesitancy and dribbling. Flank pain and urethral discharge are not typical of BPH. Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
217. In addition to the general symptoms of anaemia, a patient with pernicious anaemia also manifest with: -
A. Coagulation deficiencies
B. Low immunity
C. Neurological symptoms
D. Cardiovascular disturbances
Answer: C. Neurological symptoms
Rationale: Pernicious anaemia results from vitamin B12 deficiency, which is essential for myelin; deficiency causes neurological manifestations such as paraesthesia, ataxia and impaired proprioception (subacute combined degeneration) in addition to general anaemia symptoms. Brunner & Suddarth.
218. Rapid and deeper respirations are stimulated by the respiratory centre of the brain when:
A. Oxygen saturation levels are greater than 90%
B. Alveoli contract
C. Carbon dioxide levels increase
D. The diaphragm contracts and lowers its dome
Answer: C. Carbon dioxide levels increase
Rationale: The respiratory centre is chiefly driven by rising arterial CO2 (and resulting H+ via central chemoreceptors), stimulating faster, deeper breathing to eliminate CO2. Respiratory physiology, anatomy & physiology texts.
219. A client with infective endocarditis is receiving intravenous antibiotics. An essential nursing intervention would be to;
A. Monitor temperature trends and blood cultures
B. Restrict fluid intake below one liter daily
C. Encourage strenuous exercise to improve strength
D. Maintain complete bed rest throughout therapy
Answer: A. Monitor temperature trends and blood cultures
Rationale: In infective endocarditis on IV antibiotics, monitoring temperature trends and serial blood cultures assesses response to therapy and clearance of the infection, guiding treatment. Fluid restriction, strenuous exercise and mandatory complete bed rest are not indicated. Brunner & Suddarth.
220. The primary reason for administering digoxin (Lanoxin) to a patient is to:-
A. Relax the walls of the heart’s arteries.
B. Improve the strength of the heart beat.
C. Prevent irregularities in ventricular contractions.
D. Decrease inflammation of the heart wall.
Answer: B. Improve the strength of the heart beat.
Rationale: Digoxin is a positive inotrope that increases myocardial contractility (force of contraction) while slowing conduction; its primary purpose in heart failure is to strengthen the heartbeat. Kenya pharmacology guidelines/Brunner & Suddarth.
221. The frontal lobe of the brain is
A. The primary receptive area for vision
B. Mainly responsible for auditory sensation and coordination
C. Centre for respiratory and vasomotor activity
D. Responsible for abstract thinking, judgement and emotions
Answer: D. Responsible for abstract thinking, judgement and emotions
Rationale: The frontal lobe governs higher executive functions including abstract thinking, judgement, personality, voluntary motor control and emotional regulation. Vision is occipital, hearing is temporal, and respiratory/vasomotor centres are in the brainstem. Anatomy & physiology texts.
222. The purpose of monitoring central venous (CVP) for the critically ill patients is to,
A. Help assess the functioning of left side of the heart
B. Monitor fluid replacement therapy
C. Estimate the levels of pulmonary pressures in the critically ill
D. Help assess functioning of the mitral and tricuspid valves
Answer: B. Monitor fluid replacement therapy
Rationale: CVP reflects right atrial/right heart filling pressure and is used to guide and monitor fluid replacement therapy and right-sided cardiac function in the critically ill. Left-sided assessment requires pulmonary artery wedge pressure. Brunner & Suddarth critical care nursing.
223. Post thyroidectomy, the nurse can rule out laryngeal nerve damage by: -
A. Asking the patient to speak.
B. Stimulating the patient’s gag reflex.
C. Determining the patient’s ability to swallow.
D. Telling the patient to extend his tongue.
Answer: A. Asking the patient to speak.
Rationale: The recurrent laryngeal nerve supplies the vocal cords, so hoarseness or voice changes after thyroidectomy indicate nerve injury. Asking the patient to speak assesses voice quality and thus laryngeal nerve integrity (Brunner & Suddarth). Gag/swallow tests assess other functions.
224. A patient with heart failure develops sudden frothy pink sputum and severe dyspnea. The first  action the nurse should perform first is;
A. Place patient in high Fowler's position
B. Obtain detailed dietary assessment history
C. Encourage oral fluid intake immediately
D. Prepare patient for discharge planning
Answer: A. Place patient in high Fowler's position
Rationale: Frothy pink sputum with severe dyspnea signals acute pulmonary oedema. The immediate priority is positioning the patient upright (high Fowler's) to reduce venous return and improve ventilation while oxygen and drugs are prepared (Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya MOH). Fluids and dietary history are inappropriate.
225. Intermittent bubbling in the water seal chamber of an underwater seal drainage is,
A. Normal outcome
B. Indicative of an air leak.
C. Indicative of a pneumothorax resolution.
D. Expected when the unit is attached to wall suction.
Answer: A. Normal outcome
Rationale: Intermittent bubbling in the water seal chamber reflects air leaving the pleural space during expiration/coughing and is a normal, expected finding. Continuous bubbling would indicate an air leak (Brunner & Suddarth chest drainage).
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
226. The Independent functioning model was formulated by;
A. Virginia Henderson
B. Calista Roy
C. Florence Nightngale
D. Dorothea Orem
Answer: D. Dorothea Orem
Rationale: Dorothea Orem developed the Self-Care Deficit theory, which emphasises independent self-care functioning of the patient. Henderson's is the Needs/complementary-supplementary model, Roy's is Adaptation, and Nightingale's is Environmental (Kozier; nursing theory texts).
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
227. Prothrombin and fibrinogen are produced by: -
A. Liver
B. Pancreas
C. Bone marrow
D. Gall bladder
Answer: A. Liver
Rationale: Prothrombin and fibrinogen are plasma clotting factors synthesised by the liver, which also requires vitamin K for several factors. This is why liver disease causes coagulopathy (Guyton/Anatomy & Physiology).
228. A patient with hypertension is prescribed enalapril. Which adverse effect should the nurse monitor the patient for;
A. Severe constipation and urinary retention
B. Persistent dry cough and hypotension
C. Bradycardia and excessive salivation episodes
D. Visual disturbances and hearing impairment
Answer: B. Persistent dry cough and hypotension
Rationale: Enalapril is an ACE inhibitor; the classic adverse effects are a persistent dry cough (from bradykinin accumulation) and hypotension (first-dose and dose-related). Hyperkalaemia and angioedema also occur (Kenya Pharmacology guidelines; BNF).
229. Post below below-knee amputation in a patient with diabetes poses the risk of:-
A. Hemorrhage
B. Edema of the stump
C. Contractures
D. Wound dehiscence
Answer: A. Hemorrhage
Rationale: In a diabetic patient (peripheral vascular disease, fragile vessels), the immediate post-operative complication following below-knee amputation is haemorrhage from the stump, which is why a tourniquet is kept at the bedside. Brunner & Suddarth lists haemorrhage as the priority early post-op risk requiring the nurse to monitor the dressing and have haemostatic measures ready. Contractures are a later rehabilitation concern, not the acute post-op risk implied by 'post-amputation.'
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
230. Addison’s disease results form: -
A. Decreased production of parathyroid hormone.
B. Excessive secretion of epinephrine and norepinephrine
C. Inadequate secretion of glucocorticoids
D. Over activity of parathyroid glands
Answer: C. Inadequate secretion of glucocorticoids
Rationale: Addison's disease is primary adrenocortical insufficiency, characterised by inadequate secretion of glucocorticoids (cortisol) and mineralocorticoids (aldosterone). This causes hypotension, hyperpigmentation and electrolyte disturbance (Brunner & Suddarth).
231. Microorganisms that have both bacterial and viral characteristics include:
A. Mycoplasma, spirochetes
B. Rickettsia, chlamydia
C. Plasmodium, mycoplasma
D. Spirochetes, chlamydia
Answer: B. Rickettsia, chlamydia
Rationale: Rickettsia and Chlamydia are obligate intracellular bacteria that share features with viruses (intracellular dependence on host cells) while retaining bacterial characteristics such as cell walls and susceptibility to antibiotics (Microbiology). This makes them the classic 'in-between' organisms.
232. An assessment finding that is most indicative of cardiogenic shock is;
A. Warm skin and bounding peripheral pulses
B. Hypertension and bradycardia after exertion
C. Hypotension with cool clammy extremities
D. Elevated temperature with flushed appearance
Answer: C. Hypotension with cool clammy extremities
Rationale: Cardiogenic shock produces reduced cardiac output leading to hypotension with cool, clammy, poorly perfused extremities, tachycardia and oliguria. Warm skin/bounding pulses suggest distributive (septic) shock (Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya emergency care).
233. A patient with common bile duct obstruction related to cancer pancreas is likely to present with: -
A. Brown faeces, dark coloured urine
B. Scleral icterus, malena stool
C. Dark coloured urine, scleral icterus
D. Jaundice, brown faeces
Answer: C. Dark coloured urine, scleral icterus
Rationale: Obstruction of the common bile duct (e.g. by carcinoma of the head of pancreas) causes obstructive/post-hepatic jaundice: conjugated bilirubin cannot reach the gut, so it spills into blood and urine giving dark urine and scleral icterus (jaundice), while stools become pale/clay-coloured (not brown) due to absent stercobilin. Melaena is not a feature. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
234. The management of a child with severe malnutrition includes;
A. Warmth, antibiotics, analgesics, dietary education
B. Intensive feeding regime with high energy milk 2hourly, antibiotics, warmth
C. Intensive feeding regime 3 hourly, multivitamin syrup for one month, steroids
D. IV dextrose 50% stat then normal saline, blood transfusion, oxygen administration
Answer: B. Intensive feeding regime with high energy milk 2hourly, antibiotics, warmth
Rationale: Kenya IMNCI and WHO/MOH severe acute malnutrition protocol emphasises frequent (2-hourly) feeds with therapeutic milk (F75 in stabilisation), routine broad-spectrum antibiotics, and prevention of hypothermia (warmth), alongside management of hypoglycaemia and dehydration. Steroids and high-dextrose/blood transfusion as routine are inappropriate. Per Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols.
235. A patient with chronic kidney disease suddenly develops chest pain and peaked T waves. As the nurse caring for the patient, you will suspect?
A. Hyperkalemia
B. Hypocalcemia
C. Hypoglycemia
D. Hypernatremia
Answer: A. Hyperkalemia
Rationale: Chronic kidney disease impairs potassium excretion causing hyperkalaemia, whose hallmark ECG change is tall, peaked T waves; associated cardiac irritability can cause arrhythmia and chest symptoms. This is a life-threatening electrolyte emergency. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
236. For a patient with cellulitis, the nurse should: -
A. Apply warm compresses to the site and elevate affected extremity
B. Apply cold compresses to the site and elevate affected extremity
C. Administer topical antibiotics and oral analgesics as prescribed
D. Administer plasma expanders and elevate the affected limb to 90 degrees
Answer: A. Apply warm compresses to the site and elevate affected extremity
Rationale: Nursing care of cellulitis includes warm, moist compresses to increase circulation and promote resolution, plus elevation of the affected limb to reduce oedema, along with prescribed systemic antibiotics and analgesia. Cellulitis requires systemic (not merely topical) antibiotics. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
237. Patients with ulcerative colitis are put on bed rest mainly to: -
A. Prevent falls that can cause massive bleeding
B. Reduce oxygen demand
C. Reduce intestinal peristalsis
D. Conserve energy
Answer: C. Reduce intestinal peristalsis
Rationale: In ulcerative colitis bed rest and reduced activity help decrease intestinal motility/peristalsis, resting the inflamed bowel and reducing diarrhoea and cramping. This bowel rest is central to conservative management of the acute flare. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
238. The causes of pre-renal failure include: -
A. Hemorrhage, burns and dehydration
B. Tumors, Trauma and chemical toxicity
C. Renal caliculi, benign prostatic hypertrophy and blood clots
D. Tubular necrosis, septic shock and hemolytic blood reaction transfusion
Answer: A. Hemorrhage, burns and dehydration
Rationale: Pre-renal failure results from reduced renal perfusion, caused by hypovolaemia/decreased effective circulating volume such as haemorrhage, burns and dehydration. Tumours/calculi/BPH cause post-renal obstruction, while tubular necrosis and haemolytic transfusion reactions cause intrinsic (renal) failure. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
239. The primary rationales for tube feeding for a client post total laryngectomy due to cancer of larynx is to:-
A. Meet dietary needs and prevent fistula formation
B. Maintain an open airway and prevent aspiration
C. Meet fluid & dietary needs and prevent aspiration
D. Prevent fistula formation and reduce aspiration
Answer: C. Meet fluid & dietary needs and prevent aspiration
Rationale: After total laryngectomy the pharynx/suture line must heal; tube (nasogastric) feeding meets fluid and nutritional needs while bypassing the oral/pharyngeal route to prevent aspiration and protect the suture line during healing. The airway is separately secured by the tracheostomy stoma. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
240. The correct management for a patient with an atrial fibrillation that has clinically manifested for 12hrs is: -
A. Cardioversion.
B. Defibrillation
C. Cardioversion and defibrillation
D. Pacing.
Answer: A. Cardioversion.
Rationale: For symptomatic atrial fibrillation of recent onset (within about 48 hours), synchronised electrical (or pharmacological) cardioversion is used to restore sinus rhythm. Defibrillation is for pulseless VT/VF, not AF; pacing treats bradyarrhythmias. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing / ACLS.
241. Nursing interventions for a patient with multiple myeloma include;
A. Providing plenty of oral fluids
B. Catheterization to monitor urine output
C. Encouraging weight bearing exercises
D. Providing low calorie diet in small frequent amounts
Answer: A. Providing plenty of oral fluids
Rationale: In multiple myeloma, generous hydration (about 3 litres/day) is essential to prevent hypercalcaemia-related complications and myeloma cast nephropathy by promoting calcium and light-chain excretion. Weight-bearing must be cautious due to bone lysis/fracture risk, and diet should be normal-to-high calorie. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
242. In caring for a skin graft site, the nurse should: -
A. Perform the first dressing change after 2 to 5 days.
B. Nurse grafted extremity in a neutral position.
C. Apply an occlusive dressing daily till the graft takes.
D. Daily apply sterile saline compresses to prevent drying of the graft.
Answer: A. Perform the first dressing change after 2 to 5 days.
Rationale: A skin graft must be left undisturbed for the first few days to allow revascularisation (imbibition then inosculation); the initial dressing is therefore typically left intact and first changed around days 3-5 to avoid dislodging the graft. The extremity is immobilised/elevated (not merely neutral), and grafts are kept moist but not allowed to dry. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
243. Management of osteoporosis include: -
A. Increased calcium intake, decreased coffee intake
B. Performing weight bearing exercises, Increased calcium intake
C. Decreased calcium intake, engaging in regular moderate activity
D. Cessation of smoking, avoiding weight bearing exercises
Answer: B. Performing weight bearing exercises, Increased calcium intake
Rationale: Osteoporosis management centres on increasing calcium and vitamin D intake and performing regular weight-bearing exercise to stimulate bone formation and slow bone loss (Brunner & Suddarth). Both elements in this option are correct; calcium should be increased, not decreased, so options C is wrong, and weight-bearing exercise should be encouraged, so D is wrong.
244. Turbid urine is an indication of: -
A. Dehydration and urinary tract infection.
B. Presence of prostatic fluid and White Blood Count.
C. Anuria and presence of RBCs.
D. Perineal injury and prolonged use of diuretics
Answer: A. Dehydration and urinary tract infection.
Rationale: Turbid (cloudy) urine is classically caused by pus, bacteria and cells in urinary tract infection and by concentrated urine in dehydration (Kozier Fundamentals). These are the recognised common causes of turbidity, making option A the best answer.
245. The best fluid to administer to a patient after gastrointestinal fluid loss is: -
A. Normal saline
B. 5% dextrose
C. 10% dextrose
D. Ringer’s lactate
Answer: D. Ringer’s lactate
Rationale: After gastrointestinal fluid loss, electrolytes (Na, K, Cl, bicarbonate) are lost along with water, so a balanced isotonic crystalloid such as Ringer's lactate best replaces both fluid and electrolytes (Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya fluid guidelines). Normal saline lacks potassium and bicarbonate, and dextrose solutions do not adequately replace electrolytes.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
246. To prevent side effects associated with prolonged phenytoin use, a client should be educated to: -
A. Avoid crowds and obtain influenza vaccination annually.
B. Drink 2L of fluid daily including 6-8 glasses of water.
C. Eat a potassium rich, low sodium diet.
D. Observe good dental hygiene and report any gum bleeding.
Answer: D. Observe good dental hygiene and report any gum bleeding.
Rationale: Phenytoin commonly causes gingival hyperplasia, so clients are taught meticulous oral/dental hygiene and to report gum overgrowth or bleeding (Brunner & Suddarth pharmacology). The other options relate to unrelated drug side effects.
247. For a patient to undergo a kidney, ureter and bladder X-ray is important for the nurse to know that: -
A. There is no special preparation required.
B. Oral fluids must be withheld the morning of the examination.
C. The patient should have a full bladder before the procedure.
D. An enema should be administered before the procedure
Answer: A. There is no special preparation required.
Rationale: A plain KUB (kidney, ureter, bladder) X-ray is a simple radiograph requiring no contrast, fasting, bowel prep or bladder filling (Brunner & Suddarth diagnostic nursing). Therefore no special preparation is needed.
248. To administer Normal saline 500mls for 6hrs using an IV administration set with a drop factor of 20, the flow rate will be regulated to:
A. 26 drops per minute
B. 25 drops per minute
C. 28 drops per minute
D. 27 drops per minute
Answer: C. 28 drops per minute
Rationale: Flow rate = (volume x drop factor)/time in minutes = (500 x 20)/(6 x 60) = 10000/360 = 27.8, which rounds to 28 drops per minute (standard IV drip calculation, Kozier). This gives the closest whole-number rate.
249. Immediately post-operatively a nurse should,
A. Position a patient in recovery position, measure urine output and assess respirations every 15 minutes
B. Maintain patent airway, measure central venous pressure (CVP) and check oxygen saturation levels
C. Position patient in recovery position, observe adverse effects of anesthesia and assess reflexes
D. Check IV site for infiltration, administer clear oral fluids and monitor vital signs every 15 minutes
Answer: C. Position patient in recovery position, observe adverse effects of anesthesia and assess reflexes
Rationale: Immediately post-operatively the priority is airway protection by placing the unconscious/semi-conscious patient in the recovery (lateral) position, monitoring for anaesthetic complications and assessing return of protective reflexes (Brunner & Suddarth peri-operative nursing). This option best captures immediate recovery-phase priorities without invasive CVP monitoring or premature oral fluids.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
250. The stage of dying process according to Kubler Ross where the patient cries a lot as though mourning is: -
A. Bargaining
B. Depression
C. Anger
D. Acceptanc
Answer: B. Depression
Rationale: In Kubler-Ross's stages of dying, depression is the stage where the patient becomes withdrawn, sad and cries as though mourning the anticipated loss (Kozier; psychosocial nursing). This matches the described behaviour better than anger or bargaining.
251. The most important nursing intervention for a patient with a skin disorder is: -
A. Patient teaching
B. Prevention of secondary infection
C. Application of infection
D. Counselling because of altered body image.
Answer: B. Prevention of secondary infection
Rationale: In skin disorders the broken skin barrier predisposes to secondary bacterial infection, so the priority nursing intervention is preventing secondary infection to promote healing and avoid complications (Brunner & Suddarth dermatologic nursing). Teaching and counselling are important but subordinate to protecting compromised skin integrity.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
252. A child on long term use of corticosteroids is at risk of: -
A. Growth retardation.
B. Peripheral neuropathy.
C. Muscular degeneration.
D. Hyperkalemia.
Answer: A. Growth retardation.
Rationale: Long-term corticosteroid use in children suppresses growth by inhibiting growth hormone effects and epiphyseal activity, causing growth retardation (Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya paediatric pharmacology). Corticosteroids cause hypokalaemia, not hyperkalaemia, so option D is wrong.
253. he type of seizures which may only affect a finger or hand is: -
A. Petit mal seizure
B. Complex partial seizure
C. Absent seizure
D. Simple partial seizure
Answer: D. Simple partial seizure
Rationale: A simple partial (focal) seizure arises from a localized area of one cerebral hemisphere and may produce isolated motor activity in a single body part such as a finger or hand, without loss of consciousness. Absence and petit mal seizures are generalized, and complex partial seizures involve impaired awareness. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
254. Buck’s traction is: -
A. A form of skin traction and is often used to relieve muscle spasms.
B. A form of skeletal traction for compound fractures.
C. Only used in correction of hip and femur fractures.
D. Always contra-indicated in obese patients.
Answer: A. A form of skin traction and is often used to relieve muscle spasms.
Rationale: Buck's traction is a type of skin (not skeletal) traction applied to the lower limb, used to immobilise fractures, relieve muscle spasm and reduce pain, commonly before hip or femur surgery. It is not for compound fractures and is not always contraindicated in obesity. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
255. A patient with meningitis suddenly develops seizures and decreasing consciousness. The priority nursing action by the nurse would be;
A. Protect airway and ensure safety
B. Administer oral medication
C. Provide dietary education
D. Assess bowel sounds
Answer: A. Protect airway and ensure safety
Rationale: During a seizure with decreasing consciousness the priority follows the ABCs: maintain a patent airway, position the patient safely to prevent injury and aspiration. Oral medication is contraindicated in a seizing/unconscious patient, and dietary education or bowel assessment are not priorities. Reference: Kozier Fundamentals; Brunner & Suddarth.
256. Whitish deposits that settle at the bottom of the specimen bottle in urinalysis are: -
A. Phosphates
B. Urates
C. Mucus
D. Pus
Answer: A. Phosphates
Rationale: Amorphous phosphates precipitate as a whitish/cloudy sediment settling at the bottom of an alkaline urine specimen and dissolve on adding acid or heat. Urates form a pinkish sediment in acidic urine, while pus and mucus have different characteristics. Reference: standard urinalysis/clinical laboratory nursing texts.
257. The appropriate health message to a patient who develops stomatitis secondary to radiotherapy is: -
A. Gurgle with mouthwash and rinse thoroughly after each meal.
B. Use ice cold liquids such as tea or cola to relieve discomfort.
C. Use a toothbrush soaked in saline to clean the mouth.
D. Drink citrus juices
Answer: C. Use a toothbrush soaked in saline to clean the mouth.
