Rationale: After bronchoscopy the throat is anaesthetised, so oral intake is withheld until the gag/cough reflex returns; resolution of hoarseness (from laryngeal instrumentation) further confirms recovery of protective airway reflexes. This prevents aspiration. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth respiratory diagnostic nursing care.
262. The risk for macrovascular complications in diabetes mellitus may be reduced by,
A. Lowering BP, avoiding nephrotoxic substances & reducing strenuous exercises.
B. Weight reduction, reducing fat/triglyceride levels, quitting smoking.
C. Foot care, early diagnosis of retinal detachment & daily U/Es checkups.
D. High fibre diet, low fat diet & prompt treatment of systemic infections
Answer: B. Weight reduction, reducing fat/triglyceride levels, quitting smoking.
Rationale: Macrovascular complications of diabetes (coronary artery disease, stroke, peripheral vascular disease) are atherosclerotic; risk is reduced by controlling modifiable cardiovascular factors—weight reduction, lowering lipids/triglycerides and smoking cessation. Foot/retinal care and U/E checks relate more to microvascular complications. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya diabetes guidelines.
263. A nursing diagnosis that should receive highest priority for a patient experiencing acute pulmonary edema is;
A. Disturbed sleep pattern related to dyspnea episodes
B. Risk for infection related to hospitalization period
C. Impaired gas exchange related to alveolar flooding
D. Deficient knowledge related to disease management
Answer: C. Impaired gas exchange related to alveolar flooding
Rationale: In acute pulmonary oedema, fluid floods the alveoli, impairing gas exchange and causing life-threatening hypoxaemia; by ABC/oxygenation priority this diagnosis takes precedence. Disturbed sleep, risk for infection and deficient knowledge are lower priority. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth cardiovascular nursing.
264. The antidote for heparin is:-
A. Vitamin K
B. Warfarin (Coumadin)
C. Thrombin
D. Protamine sulfate
Answer: D. Protamine sulfate
Rationale: Protamine sulfate is the specific antidote for heparin overdose, neutralising its anticoagulant effect by binding to the acidic heparin molecule. Vitamin K is the antidote for warfarin. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing; Kenya pharmacology guidelines.
265. The early signs of cancer of the posterior-nasal space include: -
A. Proptosis, hearing loss.
B. Hearing loss, double vision.
C. Purulent nasal discharge, aphonia.
D. Anosmia, proptosis.
Answer: B. Hearing loss, double vision.
Rationale: Nasopharyngeal (posterior-nasal space) carcinoma classically presents early with conductive hearing loss from Eustachian tube obstruction and cranial nerve involvement causing diplopia (double vision). Reference: Brunner & Suddarth ENT nursing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
266. Conductive hearing loss can be caused by:-
A. Ototoxic medication,otitis externa
B. Presbycusis,ototoxic medication
C. Meniere’s disease,otosclerosis
D. Otosclerosis,tympanic memberane retraction
Answer: D. Otosclerosis,tympanic memberane retraction
Rationale: Conductive hearing loss results from problems in the outer/middle ear such as otosclerosis (fixation of the stapes) and tympanic membrane retraction. Presbycusis, ototoxicity and Meniere's disease cause sensorineural loss. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth ENT nursing.
267. The health message given a patient following tympanoplasty is: -
A. Drink fluids using a straw
B. Avoid blowing the nose and sneeze through the mouth
C. Administer saline water into the ear to aid healing
D. Avoid noisy places up to two weeks post operatively
Answer: B. Avoid blowing the nose and sneeze through the mouth
Rationale: After tympanoplasty, patients must avoid increasing middle-ear pressure; blowing the nose or closed-mouth sneezing transmits pressure via the Eustachian tube and can disrupt the graft, so they are taught to sneeze with the mouth open. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth ENT post-operative care.
268. Normal Cerebral spinal fluid;
A. Can contain a few white blood cells
B. Tests negative for glucose and protein
C. Appears clear and turbid
D. Appears slightly cloudy and turbid
Answer: A. Can contain a few white blood cells
Rationale: Normal CSF is clear and colourless, tests positive for glucose, has minimal protein, and normally contains 0-5 lymphocytes/mm3 (a few white blood cells). It should never be turbid/cloudy. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth neurological assessment.
269. In acute heart failure with pulmonary edema, morphine is administered in small doses to:
A. Promote excretion of sodium and water by kidneys
B. Relieve hypoxemia and dyspnea
C. Delay release of calcium from intracellular reservoirs
D. Reduce peripheral resistance & venous return
Answer: D. Reduce peripheral resistance & venous return
Rationale: In acute pulmonary oedema, morphine reduces preload (venous return) and afterload (peripheral resistance) through venodilation, easing the workload on the failing heart, and also relieves anxiety. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya MOH cardiac guidelines.
270. Neurovascular assessment of the lower limb post-cast application involves observation of:
A. Color, sensation and pulses
B. Color, warmth and muscle power
C. Level of consciousness, limb symmetry and muscular strength
D. Capillary refill, sensation and muscle power
Answer: A. Color, sensation and pulses
Rationale: Neurovascular (CSM) assessment of a casted limb monitors circulation (colour, temperature, capillary refill, pulses), sensation and movement. The option giving colour, sensation and pulses best reflects the neurovascular components. Reference: Kozier Fundamentals; Brunner & Suddarth orthopaedic nursing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
271. The priority nursing diagnosis for a patient with heart failure and pulmonary oedema is
A. Risk for infection related to stasis of alveolar secretions
B. Impaired skin integrity related to pressure
C. Activity intolerance related to pump failure
D. Constipation related to immobility
Answer: C. Activity intolerance related to pump failure
Rationale: In heart failure with pulmonary oedema the failing pump reduces cardiac output and oxygenation, so activity intolerance related to pump failure/imbalance between oxygen supply and demand is the priority diagnosis addressing the core pathophysiology. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth cardiac nursing.
272. The most appropriate nursing intervention to decrease postoperative edema and pain following an inguinal herniorrhaphy is: -
A. Applying a truss to the hernia site
B. Allowing the patient to stand to void
C. Elevating the scrotum with a support
D. Supporting the incision during coughing and deep breathing
Answer: C. Elevating the scrotum with a support
Rationale: After inguinal herniorrhaphy, scrotal oedema is common; elevating and supporting the scrotum (e.g., scrotal support/ice) reduces oedema and relieves pain. A truss is not used post-repair. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth surgical nursing.
273. The clinical manifestations for carbon-monoxide poisoning include: -
A. Headache, muscular spasms, cherry-red skin color.
B. Pink skin color, muscle rigidity, paresthesia.
C. Palpitations, battle’s sign, rhinorrhea
D. Coma, bluish skin coloration, parosmia.
Answer: A. Headache, muscular spasms, cherry-red skin color.
Rationale: Carbon monoxide poisoning classically produces headache, dizziness, muscle weakness/spasms and the characteristic cherry-red skin colour due to carboxyhaemoglobin. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth emergency nursing; Kenya emergency guidelines.
274. A client with a cataract would most likely complain of:-
A. Blurred and hazy vision
B. Halos and rainbows around lights
C. Eye pain and irritation that worsens at night
D. Eye strain and headache when doing close work
Answer: A. Blurred and hazy vision
Rationale: Cataract is opacification of the lens, producing painless, progressive blurred and hazy vision as light transmission is reduced. Halos are more typical of glaucoma; pain and eye strain are not features of uncomplicated cataract (Brunner & Suddarth).
275. The sensitivity part of a culture & sensitivity test is for the purpose of:-
A. Identifying the antibiotics that the patient is resistant to
B. Testing the concentration of antibiotic required to cure an infection
C. Identifying the causative organism for the infection diagnosed
D. Identifying the most effective antibiotics against the causative organism
Answer: D. Identifying the most effective antibiotics against the causative organism
Rationale: In culture and sensitivity, culture isolates/identifies the organism while sensitivity determines which antibiotics are most effective against it, guiding targeted therapy (Kenya microbiology/pharmacology guidelines).
276. Impetigo contagiosa is: -
A. Primarily the result of allergies
B. A fungal infection
C. Best treated with benzyl benzoate
D. Caused by beta haemolytic streptococcus
Answer: D. Caused by beta haemolytic streptococcus
Rationale: Impetigo contagiosa is a contagious bacterial skin infection caused by Group A beta-haemolytic streptococcus and/or Staphylococcus aureus, not a fungal or allergic condition. Benzyl benzoate treats scabies, not impetigo (Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya paediatric protocols).
277. Nursing interventions for a patient with acute infective endocarditis include: -
A. Early ambulation and activity progression
B. Restricted activity for several weeks
C. Low calorie diet
D. Increased fluid intake
Answer: B. Restricted activity for several weeks
Rationale: Acute infective endocarditis requires prolonged rest and restricted activity for several weeks to reduce cardiac workload while the valvular infection resolves on IV antibiotics. Early ambulation would strain the infected myocardium/valves (Brunner & Suddarth).
278. In pyloric stenosis there is:-
A. Projectile vomiting & olive-sized mass in abdomen’s upper right quadrant
B. Blood tinged vomitus and significantly reduced peristaltic waves
C. Severe abdominal pain and irregular opening of the bowels
D. Normal feeding appetite and passing of loose bowel motions
Answer: A. Projectile vomiting & olive-sized mass in abdomen’s upper right quadrant
Rationale: Hypertrophic pyloric stenosis classically presents with non-bilious projectile vomiting, a palpable olive-sized mass in the right upper quadrant, and visible peristaltic waves. The infant remains hungry (Kenya basic paediatric protocols; Nelson).
279. In an overweight patient, a subcutaneous injection is given at an angle of: -
A. 45 degrees
B. 90 degrees
C. 15 degrees
D. 30 degrees
Answer: B. 90 degrees
Rationale: In an obese/overweight patient with abundant subcutaneous tissue, a subcutaneous injection is given at 90 degrees to ensure the needle reaches the SC layer. In thin patients a 45-degree angle is used (Kozier Fundamentals).
280. Nursing interventions for a patient with cholecystitis associated with cholelithiasis are based on the knowledge that: -
A. The disorder can be successfully treated with oral bile salts that dissolve gallstones.
B. Analgesics are usually not necessary to relieve the pain of bile duct spasms.
C. A heavy meal with a high fat content may worsen the signs and symptoms of the disease.
D. A low cholesterol diet is indicated to reduce the availability of cholesterol for gallstone formation.
Answer: C. A heavy meal with a high fat content may worsen the signs and symptoms of the disease.
Rationale: Fatty meals stimulate gallbladder contraction and cholecystokinin release, precipitating biliary colic in cholecystitis/cholelithiasis; hence a low-fat diet is advised. Oral bile salts are rarely effective and analgesia is required for spasm pain (Brunner & Suddarth).
281. The characteristic signs and symptoms of laryngo tracheo-bronchitis are;
A. Stridor, subglottic edema.
B. Wheeze, vomiting.
C. Chest pain, pyrexia.
D. Spasmodic cough, copious vomiting
Answer: A. Stridor, subglottic edema.
Rationale: Laryngotracheobronchitis (viral croup) is characterised by inspiratory stridor, a barking cough and subglottic oedema/narrowing. Wheeze, chest pain and copious vomiting are not typical features (Kenya basic paediatric protocols).
Year: Undated
282. A patient taking chlorpromazine reports dizziness upon standing suddenly from bed. The side effect that is most likely occurring is;
A. Orthostatic hypotension from alpha-adrenergic blockade effects
B. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome with autonomic instability features
C. Extrapyramidal rigidity from excessive dopamine suppression
D. Anticholinergic crisis involving urinary retention symptoms
Answer: A. Orthostatic hypotension from alpha-adrenergic blockade effects
Rationale: Chlorpromazine is a low-potency phenothiazine with strong alpha-adrenergic blocking activity, causing orthostatic (postural) hypotension manifesting as dizziness on standing suddenly. This is distinct from EPS, NMS or anticholinergic retention. Per Kenya pharmacology guidelines and Brunner & Suddarth.
283. A child who presents with facial edema, tea-colored urine, and hypertension following untreated sore throat infection is likely to be suffering from;
A. Acute kidney injury caused by severe dehydration episodes
B. Nephrotic syndrome causing excessive protein urinary losses
C. Urinary tract infection involving ascending bacterial invasion
D. Acute glomerulonephritis following streptococcal infection exposure
Answer: D. Acute glomerulonephritis following streptococcal infection exposure
Rationale: Facial/periorbital oedema, tea-coloured (haematuric) urine and hypertension after an untreated streptococcal sore throat are the classic triad of post-streptococcal acute glomerulonephritis, per Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols and Nelson/Brunner paediatric nephrology. Nephrotic syndrome causes heavy proteinuria without haematuria, and UTI/AKI do not fit this post-strep picture.
284. As the nurse in a paediatric ward, you will suspect increased intracranial pressure in a pediatric patient when you observe;
A. Bradycardia accompanied by irregular respiratory patterns
B. Polyuria accompanied by excessive oral fluid intake
C. Hypotension associated with reduced peripheral circulation
D. Tachycardia associated with elevated body temperature readings
Answer: A. Bradycardia accompanied by irregular respiratory patterns
Rationale: Raised intracranial pressure produces Cushing's response: bradycardia, irregular respirations and widening pulse pressure/hypertension, per Brunner & Suddarth and Kenya paediatric neuro guidelines. Tachycardia, hypotension and polyuria are not signs of rising ICP.
285. An assessment finding that strongly suggests severe respiratory distress in an infant is;
A. Mild cough during feeding
B. Occasional sneezing during sleep
C. Respiratory rate of 32 breaths/minute
D. Head bobbing with grunting respirations
Answer: D. Head bobbing with grunting respirations
Rationale: Grunting and head bobbing indicate severe respiratory distress and increased work of breathing in infants, per Kenya IMNCI/Basic Paediatric Protocols. A rate of 32/min is normal for an infant, and mild cough or occasional sneezing are not danger signs.
286. A 2-year-old child is admitted with an inspiratory stridor, barking cough, and hoarseness that worsens at night. The child is likely to be suffering from;
A. Bronchial asthma causing reversible airway hyperreactivity episodes
B. Acute bronchiolitis causing lower airway inflammation symptoms
C. Viral croup causing upper airway obstruction manifestations
D. Bacterial pneumonia producing alveolar consolidation changes
Answer: C. Viral croup causing upper airway obstruction manifestations
Rationale: Inspiratory stridor, a barking (seal-like) cough and hoarseness worsening at night in a toddler are hallmark features of viral croup (laryngotracheobronchitis), an upper airway obstruction, per Kenya paediatric protocols. Asthma and bronchiolitis cause lower-airway wheeze, and pneumonia causes consolidation without barking cough/stridor.
287. A newborn with cleft palate is at highest risk for;
A. Cyanotic episodes caused by pulmonary outflow obstruction
B. Hypertension associated with congenital renal dysfunction
C. Visual impairment associated with retinal malformations
D. Feeding difficulties resulting in aspiration complications
Answer: D. Feeding difficulties resulting in aspiration complications
Rationale: A cleft palate impairs the infant's ability to create suction, causing feeding difficulties and risk of aspiration and poor weight gain, per Kenya neonatal/paediatric and midwifery guidelines. Cyanotic cardiac episodes, renal hypertension and retinal defects are not primary cleft-palate risks.
288. A child with diarrhea is lethargic, unable to drink, and has very slow skin pinch return. According to IMCI guidelines, the child is classified as having;
A. No dehydration
B. Some dehydration
C. Severe dehydration
D. Persistent dehydration
Answer: C. Severe dehydration
Rationale: Per Kenya IMNCI, two of the following classify severe dehydration: lethargic/unconscious, sunken eyes, not able to drink or drinking poorly, and skin pinch going back very slowly. This child has lethargy, inability to drink, and very slow skin pinch, meeting the criteria for severe dehydration.
289. A 5-year-old child has burns involving the entire left arm and anterior trunk. Using the Rule of Nines for children, what is the estimated percentage of body surface area burned will be;
A. 27%
B. 31%
C. 18%
D. 22%
Answer: A. 27%
Rationale: Using the paediatric Rule of Nines, an entire arm is 9% and the anterior trunk is 18%, giving 9 + 18 = 27%. This is the standard burn surface area estimation used in emergency/surgical nursing.
290. Intussusception is classically associated with;
A. Frothy stool mixed with undigested milk particles
B. Pale greasy stool associated with malabsorption syndromes
C. Currant jelly stool containing blood and mucus secretions
D. Watery stool containing excessive green bile pigments
Answer: C. Currant jelly stool containing blood and mucus secretions
Rationale: Intussusception classically presents with red 'currant jelly' stools composed of blood and mucus, together with colicky abdominal pain and a sausage-shaped mass. This is a hallmark finding in paediatrics (Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols; Nelson Paediatrics).
291. An example of a primitive reflex that normally disappears first during infancy is;
A. Babinski reflex
B. Rooting reflex
C. Moro reflex
D. Grasp reflex
Answer: C. Moro reflex
Rationale: Among the options, the Moro reflex disappears earliest (by about 3-4 months), whereas rooting and grasp persist longer and the Babinski response remains until around 12-24 months. Thus the Moro reflex normally disappears first (Kozier/Nelson developmental milestones).
292. While caring for a child with pneumonia, you notice that the child suddenly develops reduced breath sounds and tracheal deviation. The complication you should suspect is;
A. Viral bronchiolitis producing widespread airway inflammation
B. Pleural effusion causing mediastinal structure displacement
C. Acute epiglottitis causing upper airway obstruction symptoms
D. Pulmonary tuberculosis causing chronic cavitary lung lesions
Answer: B. Pleural effusion causing mediastinal structure displacement
Rationale: Sudden reduced breath sounds with tracheal deviation in a child with pneumonia suggests accumulation of pleural fluid (parapneumonic effusion/empyema) shifting the mediastinum and trachea. Of the options, pleural effusion best fits this clinical picture. Reference: Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols.
293. A child with ventricular septal defect is most likely to present with;
A. Cyanosis during feeding without audible cardiac murmurs
B. Severe hypertension affecting upper body extremities mainly
C. Bounding pulses with delayed lower limb circulation findings
D. Harsh cardiac murmur with recurrent respiratory infections
Answer: D. Harsh cardiac murmur with recurrent respiratory infections
Rationale: VSD is an acyanotic left-to-right shunt producing a harsh holosystolic murmur and increased pulmonary blood flow, leading to recurrent respiratory infections and failure to thrive. Reference: Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols; paediatric cardiology.
294. While assessing a child with severe asthma, the nurse recognizes the following finding as the highest priority of concern;
A. Inability to speak due to worsening respiratory distress
B. Mild wheezing after administration of bronchodilator therapy
C. Persistent productive cough with thick mucus secretions
D. Reduced appetite associated with prolonged illness episodes
Answer: A. Inability to speak due to worsening respiratory distress
Rationale: Inability to complete sentences or speak indicates severe/life-threatening asthma with impending respiratory failure and is the highest priority per Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols and IMNCI danger-sign criteria. Mild wheezing after bronchodilator, productive cough and reduced appetite are less urgent.
295. You the nurse caring for a child with sickle cell disease who complains of severe limb pain after a dehydrating exposure. The child is likely to be developing;
A. Hemorrhagic crisis involving extensive blood volume loss
B. Vaso-occlusive crisis causing tissue ischemia and pain
C. Aplastic crisis involving temporary bone marrow suppression
D. Hyperhemolytic crisis causing excessive red cell destruction
Answer: B. Vaso-occlusive crisis causing tissue ischemia and pain
Rationale: Dehydration precipitates sickling and vaso-occlusion, causing painful ischemia in the limbs; this is the classic vaso-occlusive (painful) crisis per Kenya paediatric/haematology guidelines. Aplastic crisis presents with anemia (parvovirus), and hyperhemolytic/hemorrhagic patterns do not fit dehydration-triggered limb pain.
296. A priority nursing intervention while caring for a child with acute nephrotic syndrome is;
A. Encouraging unrestricted sodium intake during meal periods
B. Monitoring daily weight to evaluate fluid retention changes
C. Restricting all oral fluids regardless of hydration status
D. Promoting vigorous physical activity to reduce edema formation
Answer: B. Monitoring daily weight to evaluate fluid retention changes
Rationale: In nephrotic syndrome, daily weight is the most reliable objective measure of fluid retention and response to therapy, guiding management of oedema. Sodium is restricted (not encouraged), fluids are managed per status rather than totally withheld, and activity is modified during acute oedema. Reference: Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols; Brunner & Suddarth.
297. A priority nursing intervention while managing a child with of diabetic ketoacidosis is;
A. Initiating rapid insulin therapy with fluid replacement
B. Restricting intravenous fluids to prevent cerebral edema
C. Encouraging high-carbohydrate meals during hyperglycemia episodes
D. Delaying electrolyte monitoring until glucose normalizes fully
Answer: A. Initiating rapid insulin therapy with fluid replacement
Rationale: Priority management of paediatric DKA is fluid resuscitation together with insulin therapy to correct hyperglycaemia and acidosis, alongside electrolyte (especially potassium) monitoring. Fluids are not restricted, high-carbohydrate feeds are contraindicated, and electrolytes must be monitored promptly. Reference: Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols; Brunner & Suddarth.
298. A 10-year-old child presents to the hospital with migratory joint pain, fever, and a new cardiac murmur two weeks after untreated sore throat infection.
A. Septic arthritis caused by hematogenous bacterial dissemination
B. Osteomyelitis involving inflammatory destruction of bone tissue
C. Juvenile rheumatoid arthritis causing chronic synovial inflammation
D. Rheumatic fever following untreated streptococcal throat infection
Answer: D. Rheumatic fever following untreated streptococcal throat infection
Rationale: Migratory polyarthritis, fever and new carditis (murmur) about two weeks after untreated pharyngitis are classic Jones criteria for acute rheumatic fever, a delayed autoimmune sequela of group A streptococcal throat infection. This matches Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols and Nelson/Brunner references. Septic arthritis and osteomyelitis are localized bacterial infections without migratory arthritis or carditis.
299. A congenital heart disease that is commonly associated with Down syndrome is;
A. Patent ductus arteriosus causing persistent fetal circulation
B. Coarctation of aorta causing obstructed systemic blood flow
C. Atrioventricular septal defect with endocardial cushion abnormalities
D. Tetralogy of Fallot causing cyanotic right ventricular outflow obstruction
Answer: C. Atrioventricular septal defect with endocardial cushion abnormalities
Rationale: The congenital cardiac lesion most characteristically associated with Down syndrome (trisomy 21) is the atrioventricular septal (endocardial cushion) defect. This is well established in paediatric cardiology and Nelson/Brunner texts.
300. A finding that is characteristic of developmental dysplasia of the hip in infants is;
A. Delayed eruption of primary dentition structures
B. Hyperextension of knees during passive movement testing
C. Positive Ortolani maneuver during hip joint assessment
D. Absent Moro reflex during neurological examination procedures
Answer: C. Positive Ortolani maneuver during hip joint assessment
Rationale: Developmental dysplasia of the hip is classically detected by a positive Ortolani (and Barlow) maneuver, where a palpable/audible clunk occurs as the dislocated femoral head reduces into the acetabulum. Other signs include limited abduction and unequal skin folds (Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols; Nelson Paediatrics).
301. A behavior that is most characteristic of toddlers according to normal growth and development patterns is;
A. Strong desire for autonomy and independence activities
B. Preference for abstract reasoning and logical analysis
C. Consistent emotional regulation during stressful situations
D. Advanced understanding of complex social moral principles
Answer: A. Strong desire for autonomy and independence activities
Rationale: Toddlers (Erikson's autonomy vs shame and doubt stage, 1-3 years) characteristically assert independence, negativism and a strong desire for autonomy. Abstract reasoning, consistent emotional regulation and complex moral understanding develop much later (Kozier; Nelson Paediatrics).
302. A child with fever is unable to breastfeed, vomits everything, and has convulsions. Which IMCI classification is most appropriate?
A. Mild febrile illness
B. Very severe febrile disease
C. Malaria with no danger signs
D. Simple upper respiratory infection
Answer: B. Very severe febrile disease
Rationale: In IMCI, a child with fever who has any general danger sign (unable to breastfeed/drink, vomits everything, convulsions, lethargy/unconsciousness) is classified as very severe febrile disease, requiring urgent pre-referral treatment and referral. Danger signs override milder classifications (Kenya IMNCI/IMCI guidelines).
303. A child receiving phenobarbital for seizure control should be monitored closely for:
A. Hyperactivity associated with increased nervous stimulation
B. Polycythemia resulting from chronic medication administration
C. Respiratory depression associated with excessive sedation
D. Severe hypertension during prolonged anticonvulsant therapy
Answer: C. Respiratory depression associated with excessive sedation
Rationale: Phenobarbital is a barbiturate/CNS depressant; the major dose-related adverse effect to monitor is sedation progressing to respiratory depression. It causes sedation (not hyperactivity), and hypertension/polycythemia are not features (Kenya paediatric protocols; pharmacology).
304. According to Erikson’s psychosocial theory, an adolescent struggling with role confusion is primarily attempting to achieve;
A. Acquisition of independence from parental supervision demands
B. Mastery of productive occupational and academic activities
C. Development of trust through caregiver emotional attachment
D. Establishment of identity and personal role integration
Answer: D. Establishment of identity and personal role integration
Rationale: In Erikson's identity vs role confusion stage of adolescence, the central task is establishing a coherent personal identity and integrating roles. Role confusion reflects failure to achieve this identity (Kozier; developmental psychology).
305. Digoxin toxicity in a pediatric patient is most likely indicated by:
A. Bradycardia associated with nausea and vomiting episodes
B. Tachycardia accompanied by increased physical activity levels
C. Hypertension occurring during administration of oxygen therapy
D. Polyuria associated with excessive fluid replacement therapy
Answer: A. Bradycardia associated with nausea and vomiting episodes
Rationale: Digoxin toxicity classically presents with bradycardia and gastrointestinal symptoms such as nausea and vomiting; the nurse must check apical pulse before administration and withhold if the rate is below the age-appropriate threshold. This is standard per Kenyan paediatric pharmacology guidelines and Brunner & Suddarth.
306. A client using Eugynon as an emergency contraceptive would require a total of how many pills to complete the dose :-
A. 2
B. 4
C. 1
D. 40
Answer: B. 4
Rationale: Eugynon (a combined ethinylestradiol 50 mcg + levonorgestrel pill) used as emergency contraception (Yuzpe regimen) requires 2 pills followed by 2 pills 12 hours later, a total of 4 pills. This aligns with the Kenya National Family Planning Guidelines Yuzpe method.
307. The mechanism of action of norplant implants include:-
A. Disrupting pregnancy, inhibiting ovulation, inhibiting implantation.
B. Thickening cervical mucus making it difficult for sperm to penetrate, inhibiting ovulation, thinning the endometrium making it unsuitable for implantation.
C. Thinning the endometrium making it unsuitable for implantation, disrupting pregnancy, inhibiting ovulation.
D. Interrupting fertilization and implantation, inhibiting ovulation, thickening cervical mucus making it difficult for sperms to penetrate.
Answer: B. Thickening cervical mucus making it difficult for sperm to penetrate, inhibiting ovulation, thinning the endometrium making it unsuitable for implantation.
Rationale: Progestin-only implants (Norplant/Jadelle) act by thickening cervical mucus to impede sperm, suppressing ovulation, and thinning the endometrium. They do not disrupt an established pregnancy. Per Kenya National Family Planning Guidelines.
308. Emergency contraceptives should be administered:-
A. 72 hours before unprotected sexual intercourse.
B. 72 hours as a single dose following unprotected sexual intercourse.
C. Within 72 hours of unprotected sexual intercourse and repeated 12 hours later.
D. Within 72 hours of unprotected sexual intercourse and repeated 24 hours later.
Answer: B. 72 hours as a single dose following unprotected sexual intercourse.
Rationale: Emergency contraceptive pills (levonorgestrel) are given within 72 hours of unprotected intercourse, and the current Kenya FP guideline recommends a single 1.5 mg dose. It must follow, not precede, intercourse. The single-dose regimen is preferred over split dosing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
309. The following will result in natural passive immunity:
A. Administration of immunoglobulins
B. Administration of modified toxins
C. Movement of maternal antibodies through the placenta to the foetus
D. Infection by a disease that confer immunity against itself
Answer: C. Movement of maternal antibodies through the placenta to the foetus
Rationale: Natural passive immunity is acquired when preformed maternal antibodies (IgG) cross the placenta to the foetus (and via breast milk). Administration of immunoglobulins is artificial passive immunity, toxoids give artificial active immunity, and infection gives natural active immunity. Thus option C is correct.
310. Microgynon is an example of:
A. Biphasic pills
B. Monophasic pills
C. Triphasic pills
D. Progesterone only pills
Answer: B. Monophasic pills
Rationale: Microgynon (30 mcg ethinylestradiol + 150 mcg levonorgestrel) delivers a fixed dose of hormones throughout the active cycle, making it a monophasic combined oral contraceptive. Per Kenya National Family Planning Guidelines and pharmacology references.
311. The non-contraceptive benefits of Depo-Provera include:
A. Prevention from anaemia, protection against sexually transmitted infections
B. Protection against endometrial cancer, reduction of menstrual flow
C. Protection against sexually transmitted infections, prevention of endometrial cancer
D. Increased menstrual flow, prevention of cancer of cervix
Answer: B. Protection against endometrial cancer, reduction of menstrual flow
Rationale: DMPA (Depo-Provera) provides non-contraceptive benefits including protection against endometrial cancer and reduced/decreased menstrual bleeding (often amenorrhoea, reducing anaemia). It does NOT protect against STIs. Per Kenya National Family Planning Guidelines.
312. Psychological needs of a school child include:
A. Degree of dependence, feelings of security
B. A stable home, proper nutrition
C. Freedom from fatigue, good sight, hearing and speech
D. Affection, Clothing
Answer: A. Degree of dependence, feelings of security
Rationale: Psychological (emotional) needs of a school-age child include security, affection and appropriate independence/dependence. Nutrition, clothing and physical health (sight/hearing) are physical needs, not psychological. Per school health and child health nursing texts.
313. Antidiuretic hormone;
A. Is released by the anterior pituitary gland
B. Excessive production cause dilutional hyponatremia
C. Causes increased potassium excretion
D. Stimulates production of aldosterone
Answer: B. Excessive production cause dilutional hyponatremia
Rationale: ADH (vasopressin) is released from the posterior pituitary and promotes water reabsorption; excess (e.g. SIADH) causes water retention and dilutional hyponatraemia. It does not increase potassium excretion or stimulate aldosterone. Per Brunner & Suddarth and physiology texts.
314. The following antituberculosis drug increases metabolism of oral contraceptives;
A. Isoniazid
B. Rifampicin
C. Ethambutol
D. Pyrazinamide
Answer: B. Rifampicin
Rationale: Rifampicin is a potent hepatic enzyme (CYP450) inducer that increases metabolism of oral contraceptives, reducing their efficacy — women on rifampicin need alternative/additional contraception. Per Kenya pharmacology and TB treatment guidelines.
315. Nursing interventions for a patient with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) include;
A. Maintaining the room temperature at 370c
B. Nursing the patient in a supine position
C. Restricting fluid intake
D. Performing postural drainage with percussion and vibration daily
Answer: D. Performing postural drainage with percussion and vibration daily
Rationale: COPD nursing care aims to mobilise secretions and improve airway clearance; chest physiotherapy (postural drainage with percussion and vibration) is a recognised intervention (Brunner & Suddarth). The patient is nursed in upright/orthopneic position, fluids are encouraged to liquefy secretions, and room temperature is kept comfortable, so the other options are wrong.
316. Rheumatic endocarditis is;
A. More common in older people
B. Non infectious
C. Common in people with prosthetic heart valves
D. Caused by staphylococcus
Answer: B. Non infectious
Rationale: Rheumatic endocarditis is an inflammatory, non-infectious sequela of rheumatic fever (an autoimmune reaction to group A streptococcal pharyngitis), distinct from infective endocarditis. It is common in children and young people, not the elderly, and is not caused directly by staphylococcus (Brunner & Suddarth).
317. Management of a patient with myocardial infarction include;
A. Moderate physical activity, high calorie diet
B. Low sodium diet, low fibre diet
C. Supine positioning, high calorie diet
D. Low calorie diet, bed rest
Answer: D. Low calorie diet, bed rest
Rationale: Post-MI management includes initial bed rest to reduce myocardial oxygen demand and a low-calorie, low-sodium, low-fat diet to reduce cardiac workload and manage risk factors. High-calorie diets and moderate activity in the acute phase are contraindicated (Brunner & Suddarth).
318. Nursing diagnosis for a patient with leukaemia include;
A. Impaired gaseous exchange, hypothermia
B. Excess fluid volume, impaired skin integrity
C. Risk for injury (bleeding), impaired gaseous exchange
D. Pain , hypothermia
Answer: C. Risk for injury (bleeding), impaired gaseous exchange
Rationale: In leukaemia, thrombocytopenia gives risk for injury/bleeding, while anaemia and possible pulmonary infiltration cause impaired gaseous exchange. Leukaemia typically presents with fever (hyperthermia) not hypothermia, and skin integrity/fluid excess are not the priority diagnoses (Brunner & Suddarth).
319. Duodenal ulcers,
A. Are more common in people above 50 years
B. Are more common in females than males
C. Have a higher risk of malignancy than gastric ulcers
D. Pain is commonly experienced 3 hours after meals.
Answer: D. Pain is commonly experienced 3 hours after meals.
Rationale: Duodenal ulcer pain characteristically occurs 2-3 hours after meals (and at night) and is relieved by food, unlike gastric ulcers. Duodenal ulcers are more common in men aged 30-50, rarely become malignant, so the other options are incorrect (Brunner & Suddarth).
320. Peritonitis is commonly caused by;
A. Escherichia Coli, Klebsiella, Proteus
B. Streptococci, Escherichia Coli, Proteus
C. Staphylococci, Streptococci, Klebsiella
D. Proteus, Pseudomonas, staphylococci
Answer: A. Escherichia Coli, Klebsiella, Proteus
Rationale: Secondary peritonitis most commonly results from gut-derived Gram-negative enteric organisms, chiefly Escherichia coli, Klebsiella and Proteus following perforation of abdominal viscera. This best represents the typical bowel flora causing peritonitis (Brunner & Suddarth).
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
321. The following is a mineralocorticoid ;
A. Cortisol
B. Adrenal corticotrophic hormone
C. Aldosterone
D. Epinephrine
Answer: C. Aldosterone
Rationale: Aldosterone is the principal mineralocorticoid secreted by the adrenal cortex (zona glomerulosa), regulating sodium and potassium balance. Cortisol is a glucocorticoid, ACTH is a pituitary hormone, and epinephrine is an adrenal medulla catecholamine (Anatomy & Physiology).
322. Nephrotic syndrome presents with;
A. Increased albumin in blood, loss of protein in urine.
B. High serum cholesterol, presence of blood in urine
C. Decreased albumin in blood, high serum cholesterol
D. Loss of albumin in urine, presence of blood in urine
Answer: C. Decreased albumin in blood, high serum cholesterol
Rationale: Nephrotic syndrome is characterised by massive proteinuria leading to hypoalbuminaemia (decreased blood albumin), hyperlipidaemia (high serum cholesterol), and oedema. Haematuria is not a defining feature, and albumin is lost in urine not increased in blood (Brunner & Suddarth).
323. Risk factors to breast cancer include;
A. Late menarche, nulliparity
B. Obesity, early menopause
C. Late menopause, nulliparity
D. Early menarche, multiparity
Answer: C. Late menopause, nulliparity
Rationale: Breast cancer risk increases with prolonged oestrogen exposure: early menarche, late menopause, and nulliparity are established risk factors. Obesity is a risk factor but early menopause and multiparity are protective, making 'late menopause, nulliparity' the correct combination (Brunner & Suddarth).
324. Systemic effects of burns include;
A. Hypovolemia, generalized vasoconstriction
B. Decreased cardiac output, bronchospasm
C. Hypotension, peripheral vasodilatation
D. Decreased capillary permeability, hypovolemia
Answer: A. Hypovolemia, generalized vasoconstriction
Rationale: Major burns cause fluid shift into the interstitium producing hypovolaemia (burn shock), and the compensatory systemic response is generalised vasoconstriction. Capillary permeability increases (not decreases) and cardiac output falls, making the other options incorrect (Brunner & Suddarth).
325. Chemosis is;
A. Seeing one object as two
B. Oedema of the conjunctiva
C. Dilatation of the vasculature of the conjunctiva
D. Presence of blood in the anterior chamber of the eye
Answer: B. Oedema of the conjunctiva
Rationale: Chemosis is defined as swelling (oedema) of the conjunctiva, typically due to allergic reaction, infection or inflammation. Diplopia is seeing one object as two, conjunctival injection is dilatation of vasculature, and hyphaema is blood in the anterior chamber. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing (eye disorders).
326. A patient post eye surgery should be advised to;
A. Perform frequent coughing exercises to prevent respiratory complications
B. Perform deep breathing exercises to prevent respiratory complications
C. Lie on the affected side immediately post operatively to promote drainage of secretions
D. Take a low fibre diet
Answer: B. Perform deep breathing exercises to prevent respiratory complications
Rationale: Post eye surgery, activities that raise intraocular pressure (coughing, straining, bending, lying on the operated side) are avoided; deep breathing is encouraged to prevent respiratory complications without raising IOP. A high-fibre diet is advised to prevent constipation and straining. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth ophthalmic post-op care.
327. Meniere’s disease management include;
A. High sodium diet, antibiotic administration
B. Administration of antibiotic and antihistamine
C. Administration of antiemetic and antihistamine
D. High potassium diet and antibiotics administration
Answer: C. Administration of antiemetic and antihistamine
Rationale: Meniere's disease management is symptomatic: antihistamines (e.g. meclizine) and antiemetics control vertigo and nausea, plus a LOW-sodium diet and diuretics to reduce endolymphatic fluid. Antibiotics have no role as it is not infective. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth (inner ear disorders).
328. Post intracranial surgery a patient requires strict temperature regulation mainly to;
A. Detect infection
B. Prevent increased metabolic demand
C. Prevent hypothermia
D. Enhance cerebral perfusion
Answer: B. Prevent increased metabolic demand
Rationale: After intracranial surgery, hyperthermia increases cerebral metabolic rate and oxygen demand, worsening secondary brain injury and raising ICP; strict temperature control prevents this increased metabolic demand. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth neurosurgical/neurologic critical care.
329. Management of a patient during a seizure include;
A. Restricting the jerking movements to prevent injury
B. Inserting an oral airway to protect the tongue
C. Putting pillows and raising the side rails if the patient is in bed
D. Removing pillows and raising the side rails if the patient is in bed.
Answer: D. Removing pillows and raising the side rails if the patient is in bed.
Rationale: During a seizure, protect the patient from injury by removing pillows/objects and padding/raising side rails; movements should never be restrained and nothing (including an oral airway) should be forced into the mouth during the convulsion. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth seizure management.
330. A patient with a sprained ankle should be advised to avoid;
A. Resting the foot
B. Applying a heating pad
C. Applying an elastic compression bandage
D. Elevating the ankle on a pillow while sitting or lying down
Answer: B. Applying a heating pad
Rationale: Acute sprain management follows RICE (Rest, Ice, Compression, Elevation); heat is contraindicated in the acute phase because it increases vasodilation, bleeding and swelling. Thus the patient should AVOID applying a heating pad. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth musculoskeletal soft-tissue injury.
331. Risk for osteoporosis is greatest in;
A. A 36-year-old man who has asthma
B. A 70-year-old man who consumes alcohol
C. A sedentary 65-year-old woman who smokes cigarette
D. A 25-year-old woman who jogs
Answer: C. A sedentary 65-year-old woman who smokes cigarette
Rationale: Osteoporosis risk is highest in postmenopausal women (low oestrogen), advancing age, sedentary lifestyle and smoking - all present in this option. The young jogger has protective weight-bearing exercise. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth osteoporosis risk factors.
332. The following indicate infection in a casted extremity
A. Diminished distal pulse
B. Presence of a “hot spot” on the cast
C. Coolness and pallor of the extremity
D. Dependent edema
Answer: B. Presence of a “hot spot” on the cast
Rationale: Infection under a cast produces a localised warm area ('hot spot'), often with a foul odour and drainage staining. Diminished pulse, coolness/pallor indicate impaired circulation, not infection. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth cast care and complications.
333. Central type obesity seen in Cushing’s syndrome occurs mainly due to:
A. Accelerated protein catabolism and protein use by muscle cells
B. Increased mineral corticoid activity leading to retention of water and sodium
C. Increased mobilisation of fats especially from the extremities and deposition in other areas
D. Increased androgen secretion hence causing masculinisation
Answer: C. Increased mobilisation of fats especially from the extremities and deposition in other areas
Rationale: Excess cortisol in Cushing's syndrome causes redistribution of fat, mobilising it from the extremities and depositing it centrally (truncal obesity, moon face, buffalo hump). Protein catabolism causes muscle wasting, not central obesity. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth adrenal/Cushing's pathophysiology.
334. The following are indications for limb observations;
A. Fracture pelvis, anemia and compartment syndrome.
B. Deep venous thrombosis, malignancy and cellulitis.
C. Compartment syndrome, Laminectomy and deep venous thrombosis.
D. Post spinal anaesthesia, fracture malleolus, Tropical ulcers.
Answer: C. Compartment syndrome, Laminectomy and deep venous thrombosis.
Rationale: Limb (neurovascular) observations monitor circulation, sensation and movement in conditions threatening these - compartment syndrome, post-laminectomy (spinal cord/nerve involvement) and DVT all require limb monitoring. Anaemia does not require limb observation, making option A incorrect. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth neurovascular assessment.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
335. Post hip joint replacement (arthroplasty) the nurse should:-
A. Maintain adduction of lower limbs and external rotation
B. Educate the patient to avoid flexing the hip joint beyond 450
C. Ensure the patient takes high calorie foods on a daily basis
D. Ascertain the closed wound suction (potovac drain) isn’t vacuumed.
Answer: D. Ascertain the closed wound suction (potovac drain) isn’t vacuumed.
Rationale: After total hip arthroplasty the nurse maintains abduction (not adduction) with internal rotation, avoids hip flexion beyond 90 degrees, and ensures the closed suction drain remains patent and functioning (i.e. that suction/vacuum is not lost or the drain not blocked). Options A and B state wrong angles/positions; the drain-patency check is the correct nursing responsibility per Brunner & Suddarth orthopaedic nursing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
336. The cerebrospinal fluid,
A. Is reabsorbed into blood capillaries in the dura mater
B. Is secreted into the subarachnoid space from pia mater capillaries
C. Is found in the ventricles and subarachnoid space
D. Is found in the subarachnoid and epidural space
Answer: C. Is found in the ventricles and subarachnoid space
Rationale: CSF is produced by the choroid plexuses of the ventricles, circulates through the ventricular system and the subarachnoid space, and is reabsorbed into venous blood via the arachnoid granulations/villi (not the dura, not the epidural space, and not secreted from pia mater). Kozier/Guyton anatomy and physiology.
337. The high threshold substances that are completely reabsorbed by renal tubules include,
A. Creatinine,urea,uric acid.
B. Water, urine, plasma proteins
C. Bicarbonate,glucose,amino acids
D. Sodium,Potassium, Phosphate
Answer: C. Bicarbonate,glucose,amino acids
Rationale: High-threshold substances are almost completely reabsorbed by the renal tubules until plasma concentration exceeds the tubular threshold; these include glucose, amino acids and bicarbonate. Creatinine and urea are poorly reabsorbed (low/no threshold), so option C is correct per renal physiology.
338. A nurse should advise a patient with reflux oesophagitis to,
A. Take high roughage diet
B. Eat highly seasoned foods
C. Avoid caffeine containing drinks
D. Take high protein, low fat and high fibre diet
Answer: D. Take high protein, low fat and high fibre diet
Rationale: In reflux (GERD) oesophagitis, dietary advice includes a high-protein, low-fat, high-fibre diet because fat delays gastric emptying and lowers oesophageal sphincter tone. Caffeine avoidance is also correct, but the option describing the overall recommended diet is the single best answer; Brunner & Suddarth GI nursing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
339. When a patient with fracture of the humerus develops a Vockman’s contracture it signifies damage to,
A. Brachial artery
B. Ulnar artery
C. Ulnar nerve
D. Radial nerve
Answer: A. Brachial artery
Rationale: Volkmann's ischaemic contracture results from compromise of the brachial artery (commonly from a supracondylar humeral fracture or tight cast), causing ischaemia of the forearm flexor muscles leading to their fibrosis and contracture. Standard orthopaedic/anatomy reference.
340. Mannitol is indicated in management of,
A. Heart failure and anaemia
B. Pulmonary edema and congestive heart failure
C. Renal failure and elevated intracranial pressure
D. Elevated intraocular pressure and increased intracranial pressure
Answer: C. Renal failure and elevated intracranial pressure
Rationale: Mannitol is an osmotic diuretic used to reduce raised intracranial pressure and intraocular pressure and to promote diuresis in oliguric/acute renal failure. It is contraindicated in established pulmonary oedema/CHF. Both listed uses in option C (renal failure and raised ICP) are correct pharmacology; option D would also fit but C is the classical NCK pairing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
341. The immediate complications of a burn injury includes,
A. Hypokalemea, ischemia and acute renal failure
B. Compartment syndrome, hyperkalemea and hypovolemea
C. Hyponatremea, anaemia and hypercalcemea
D. Acute renal failure, compartment syndrome and eschar formation
Answer: B. Compartment syndrome, hyperkalemea and hypovolemea
Rationale: Immediately after a major burn there is massive fluid shift causing hypovolaemia (burn shock), cell lysis releasing potassium causing hyperkalaemia, and circumferential/deep burns with oedema causing compartment syndrome. Hypokalaemia and hypocalcaemia develop later; option B lists the correct early complications per Brunner & Suddarth burn management.
342. The drugs that are mainly indicated in management of myocardial infarction include,
A. Pethidine, buscopan and hydrocortisone
B. Morphine,oxygen and nitroglycerine
C. Pethidine, propranolol and heparin
D. Alteplase, atenolol and junior Aspirin
Answer: B. Morphine,oxygen and nitroglycerine
Rationale: Initial management of myocardial infarction follows MONA — Morphine, Oxygen, Nitroglycerine and Aspirin. Option B captures the core immediate pharmacologic/oxygen therapy. Although alteplase and aspirin (option D) are also used, morphine-oxygen-nitroglycerine is the classic MI relief combination taught in Kenyan syllabi.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
343. The following diseases/conditions are associated with high levels of calcium in blood;
A. Multiple myeloma and osteoarthritis
B. Osteoporosis and rheumatoid arthritis
C. Bone tumours and rheumatoid arthritis
D. Multiple myeloma and bone tumours
Answer: D. Multiple myeloma and bone tumours
Rationale: Hypercalcaemia is caused by conditions with increased bone resorption/osteolysis such as multiple myeloma and bone tumours (bony metastases). Osteoarthritis, osteoporosis and rheumatoid arthritis are not associated with raised serum calcium, so option D is correct per pathophysiology.
344. The technique used in nursing patients with sickle cell crisis is:
A. Isolation
B. Reverse barrier nursing.
C. Barrier nursing.
D. General nursing.
Answer: B. Reverse barrier nursing.
Rationale: Patients in sickle cell crisis are prone to infection (functional asplenia) and are managed with reverse (protective) barrier nursing to shield the immunocompromised patient from external pathogens, rather than isolating others from them. Kenyan IPC and paediatric/medical nursing practice.
345. In stage II of a pressure sore,
A. The underlying skin is hardened (indurated)
B. There is an open lesion exposing the subcutaneous tissue
C. There is reddening of the affected pressure point skin
D. Necrosis extends through the skin and subcutaneous tissue
Answer: B. There is an open lesion exposing the subcutaneous tissue
Rationale: By NPUAP/EPUAP staging, Stage II is a partial-thickness loss of dermis presenting as a shallow open ulcer or ruptured blister, i.e. the only 'open lesion' option; Stage I is non-blanchable erythema (reddening), and full-thickness necrosis extending through subcutaneous tissue is Stage III/IV (Kozier/Brunner & Suddarth). Although the option's wording overstates depth (Stage II does not truly expose subcutaneous tissue), it is the only choice describing an open partial-thickness lesion and is the intended answer. Reddening (C) is Stage I and induration/full-thickness necrosis are other stages.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
346. When nursing a patient with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, the nurse should,
A. Ensure the patient gets high flow rate oxygen at 100%
B. Advise the patient to get influenza and pneumococcal vaccines
C. Avoid administration of prophylactic bronchodilators
D. Position the patient in dorsal recumbent position to enhance ventilation
Answer: B. Advise the patient to get influenza and pneumococcal vaccines
Rationale: COPD patients are prone to respiratory infections that trigger exacerbations, so annual influenza and pneumococcal vaccination is a standard preventive measure. High-flow 100% oxygen is dangerous (risk of CO2 narcosis by suppressing hypoxic drive), bronchodilators are indicated not avoided, and the patient is best nursed upright/orthopneic. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; MOH clinical guidelines.
347. The most important goal of nursing care for a client who is in shock is,
A. Manage fluid overload
B. Manage increased cardiac output
C. Manage inadequate tissue perfusion
D. Manage vasoconstriction of vascular beds
Answer: C. Manage inadequate tissue perfusion
Rationale: Shock is fundamentally a state of inadequate tissue perfusion and cellular oxygenation; the paramount goal is restoring adequate perfusion to vital organs. Cardiac output is typically decreased (not increased) and fluid overload is not the primary concern in most shock states. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
348. The common side-effects of anti-neoplastic therapy includes,
A. Alopecia,bone marrow depression,central nervous system depression.
B. Central nervous system depression,anorexia, alopecia.
C. Nausea and vomiting,alopecia,central nervous system depression.
D. Stomatitis,bone marrow depression,alopecia.
Answer: D. Stomatitis,bone marrow depression,alopecia.
Rationale: Antineoplastic agents characteristically damage rapidly dividing cells, producing alopecia, bone marrow depression (myelosuppression) and mucositis/stomatitis, along with nausea and vomiting. CNS depression is not a hallmark common side-effect of most cytotoxic chemotherapy, so the option listing stomatitis, bone marrow depression and alopecia is the most accurate grouping. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya Pharmacology guidelines.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
349. The following complication is associated with prolonged mechanical ventilation of a patient,
A. Gastro-intestinal haemorrhage
B. Immunosupression
C. Increased cardiac output
D. Pulmonary embolism
Answer: A. Gastro-intestinal haemorrhage
Rationale: Prolonged mechanical ventilation predisposes to stress ulceration of the gastric mucosa (Curling-type stress ulcers) leading to GI haemorrhage, which is why stress-ulcer prophylaxis is standard in ICU. It reduces cardiac output (not increases), and immunosuppression and pulmonary embolism are not its characteristic direct complications. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Critical Care Nursing.
350. A patient with a cataract is likely to complain of,
A. Blurred and hazy vision
B. Halos and rainbows around lights
C. Eye pain and irritation that worsens at night
D. Eye strain and headache when reading closely
Answer: A. Blurred and hazy vision
Rationale: Cataract causes progressive clouding of the lens producing painless blurred, hazy or cloudy vision and glare. Halos/rainbows around lights and eye pain are features of acute glaucoma, not cataract. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya ophthalmic nursing guidelines.
351. The following nursing diagnosis would be most appropriate for a client, who has undergone hysterectomy,
A. Imbalanced nutrition; less than body requirement related to nausea and vomiting
B. Excess fluid volume related to surgery
C. Ineffective breathing pattern related to post-operative pneumonia
D. Ineffective coping related to body image disturbance
Answer: D. Ineffective coping related to body image disturbance
Rationale: Hysterectomy removes the uterus, which many women associate with femininity and reproductive identity, commonly causing altered body image and grief, making ineffective coping related to body image disturbance the most appropriate psychosocial diagnosis. The other options describe complications not specifically characteristic of this procedure. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kozier Fundamentals.
352. The following finding is suggestive of a pneumothorax in a patient with chest trauma;
A. Absent breath sounds
B. Inspiratory wheezing
C. Dullness on chest percussion
D. Pronounced crackles
Answer: A. Absent breath sounds
Rationale: Pneumothorax collapses the lung so air no longer moves in that region, producing absent (diminished) breath sounds with hyper-resonance on percussion. Dullness suggests fluid/haemothorax, and wheezing/crackles indicate airway or parenchymal pathology. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya emergency care guidelines.
353. When preparing a patient for colonoscopy procedure the nurse should,
A. Perform a high colonic washout procedure the night before the procedure
B. Ensure the patient takes a high roughage diet with a laxative the night before procedure
C. Educate the patient to report any “needle prick” sensations within 12 hrs after the procedure
D. Advise the patient to take low fiber diet for 1 week before the procedure
Answer: A. Perform a high colonic washout procedure the night before the procedure
Rationale: Adequate bowel preparation/cleansing to empty the colon is essential for clear visualization during colonoscopy; a low-residue/clear-fluid diet with bowel-cleansing (washout/purgative) is done the day before. A high-roughage diet or a low-fibre diet for a whole week is incorrect, and needle-prick sensations are not a relevant post-procedure teaching point. Of the options, bowel cleansing the night before is the correct preparation. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth; Kenya endoscopy nursing guidelines.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
354. A client with a cataract would most likely complain of,
A. Halos and rainbows around lights
B. Eye pain and irritation that worsens at night
C. Blurred and hazy vision
Answer: C. Blurred and hazy vision
Rationale: A cataract is opacification of the lens, producing progressive painless blurred and hazy (cloudy) vision, per Brunner & Suddarth. Halos around lights are characteristic of glaucoma, and eye pain is not a feature of uncomplicated cataract.
355. When a burn occurs in an adult,
A. Using rule of nine, right arm is calculated as being 18% & right leg 9%.
B. Capillaries & small vessels dilate, renal blood flow reduces
C. Fluid shifts from ICF to ECF space/compartment along with potassium.
D. Permeability of the kidney tubules increases with oliguria
Answer: C. Fluid shifts from ICF to ECF space/compartment along with potassium.
Rationale: In the emergent/resuscitation phase of a burn, increased capillary permeability causes fluid and potassium to shift out of the intracellular compartment into the extracellular space, producing hyperkalaemia and hypovolaemia (Brunner & Suddarth). The rule-of-nines option is incorrect (arm=9%, leg=18%), and burns cause vasoconstriction/reduced renal flow with oliguria but decreased tubular function, so option C is the accurate physiological statement.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
356. The following technique should be avoided when changing a hemiparalysis clients’ position in bed;
A. Sliding the client to move her up in bed
B. Rolling the client onto her side
C. Lifting the client when moving her up in bed
D. Having the client lift herself off the bed using a trapeze
Answer: A. Sliding the client to move her up in bed
Rationale: Sliding/dragging a hemiparetic client produces friction and shear that damage skin and predispose to pressure injury, so it must be avoided; the client should be lifted clear of the surface (Kozier Fundamentals). Rolling, lifting, and self-assisted trapeze use are acceptable safe techniques.
357. When a blood pressure (BP) reading is abnormal upon initial assessment, it is best to check the BP,
A. On the other arm
B. On both arms sitting and standing
C. With the patient standing
D. After a 5 min wait
Answer: A. On the other arm
Rationale: When an initial BP is abnormal, standard technique is to repeat on the opposite arm to confirm the reading and detect inter-arm differences (Kozier Fundamentals; MOH guidelines). Orthostatic/standing measurements are done for specific indications, not as the first confirmatory step.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
358. The hormones responsible for blood calcium levels are:
A. Calcitonin and parathyroid hormone
B. Estrogen and progesterone
C. Melatonin and follicle stimulating hormone
D. Thyroxine and parathyroid hormone
Answer: A. Calcitonin and parathyroid hormone
Rationale: Blood calcium is regulated principally by parathyroid hormone (raises serum calcium) and calcitonin (lowers serum calcium) from the thyroid C-cells, per standard physiology (Anatomy & Physiology). Thyroxine, oestrogen, and the other hormones are not the primary calcium regulators.
359. The following manifestation of rheumatic fever can lead to permanent complications
A. Sydenham’s chorea
B. Erythema marginatum
C. Subcutaneous nodules
D. Carditis
Answer: D. Carditis
Rationale: Carditis is the only manifestation of rheumatic fever that causes permanent damage, chiefly chronic valvular heart disease (rheumatic heart disease), per Brunner & Suddarth and Kenya paediatric protocols. Chorea, erythema marginatum and subcutaneous nodules resolve without lasting sequelae.
360. The type of blood that is called the universal donor blood is:
A. Type A
B. Type B
C. Type AB
D. Type O
Answer: D. Type O
Rationale: Type O (specifically O negative) lacks A and B surface antigens, so it can be transfused to recipients of any ABO group and is termed the universal donor (Brunner & Suddarth; Anatomy & Physiology). AB is the universal recipient.
361. Movement of an extremity away from the midline of the body is called
A. Adduction
B. Pronation
C. Flexion
D. Abduction
Answer: D. Abduction
Rationale: Abduction is movement of a limb away from the midline of the body, whereas adduction is movement toward the midline (Anatomy & Physiology). Pronation and flexion describe different types of joint movement.
362. When a patient is receiving heparin therapy, the nurse should
A. Observe him for cyanosis
B. Remember that a sedimentation rate is ordered for monitoring blood coagulation
C. Give the injection intramuscularly
D. Observe emesis, urine, and stools for blood
Answer: D. Observe emesis, urine, and stools for blood
Rationale: Heparin is an anticoagulant, so the nurse must monitor for bleeding by observing emesis, urine and stool for blood (Pharmacology; Brunner & Suddarth). Heparin is monitored by aPTT (not sedimentation rate) and is given IV or SC, not IM, because of haematoma risk.
363. An abnormal condition in which there’s discomfort in breathing in any but an erect sitting position is?
A. Orthopnea
B. Dyspnea
C. Orthopsia
D. Cheyne-stokes
Answer: A. Orthopnea
Rationale: Orthopnea is the abnormal condition in which breathing is difficult or uncomfortable in any position except sitting upright or standing erect. It is a classic sign of left ventricular failure/pulmonary congestion (Brunner & Suddarth). Dyspnea is general difficult breathing; Cheyne-Stokes is a periodic breathing pattern.
364. Because the small intestine needs bile only a few times a day, bile is stored and concentrated in the:
A. Pancreas
B. Gall bladder
C. Liver
D. Small intestine
Answer: B. Gall bladder
Rationale: Bile is produced by the liver but stored and concentrated in the gall bladder, from which it is released into the small intestine when needed for fat digestion (Anatomy & Physiology; Brunner & Suddarth).
365. An appropriate nursing intervention for a 32-yr-old patient in skeletal traction would be to?
A. Provide cast care
B. Cleanse pin sites daily with hydrogen peroxide and observe for signs of infection
C. Place patient on drainage and secretion precautions
D. Encourage patient to sit in a straight, firm chair for no longer than 20 minutes each time
Answer: B. Cleanse pin sites daily with hydrogen peroxide and observe for signs of infection
Rationale: Skeletal traction uses pins/wires through bone, so pin-site care to prevent infection is the appropriate nursing intervention. Cast care and drainage precautions do not apply, and a patient in traction must remain in proper alignment, not sit in a chair (Brunner & Suddarth, orthopaedic nursing).
366. Select the most appropriate nursing assessment for the nursing diagnosis of ineffective tissue perfusion, secondary to fractured hip
A. Assess for ecchymosis over pelvis and perineum
B. Protect patient from cross-contamination
C. Assess for adventitious lung sounds
D. Assess distal pulses
Answer: D. Assess distal pulses
Rationale: For ineffective tissue perfusion secondary to a fractured hip, the key assessment is neurovascular status distal to the injury, especially distal pulses, to detect compromised circulation. Lung sounds and ecchymosis do not directly assess distal perfusion (Brunner & Suddarth).
367. A patient reports being allergic to penicillin. Which question would elicit the most useful information?
A. When did the reaction occur?
B. What infection did you have that required penicillin?
C. What type of allergic reaction did you have?
D. Did you notify your physician of the allergy?
Answer: C. What type of allergic reaction did you have?
Rationale: Determining the type/nature of the reaction distinguishes a true, potentially life-threatening allergy (e.g., anaphylaxis, rash) from a non-allergic side effect, which is the most useful information for safe medication administration (Pharmacology/Kozier).
368. The nurse is assessing the bowel sounds of her patient who had prostatectomy 2 days ago. To determine that he does not have bowel sounds present, the nurse would have to auscultate each quadrant for ;
A. 1 minute
B. 3 minutes
C. 10 minutes
D. 15 minutes
Answer: C. 10 minutes
Rationale: To conclude that bowel sounds are truly absent, the nurse must auscultate each quadrant for a full 5 to 10 minutes; of the options given, 10 minutes is required before documenting absent bowel sounds (Kozier/Brunner health assessment).
369. The nurse determines that a patient is unconscious when the patient
A. Has cerebral ischemia
B. Responds only to painful stimuli
C. Is unaware of self or the environment
D. Does not respond to verbal stimuli
Answer: C. Is unaware of self or the environment
Rationale: Unconsciousness is defined as a state in which the patient is unaware of self and the environment. Responding to painful stimuli or not responding to verbal stimuli describes levels within altered consciousness, and cerebral ischemia is a cause, not the definition (Brunner & Suddarth neurological nursing).
370. The blood that is pumped from the left ventricle contains
A. A full supply of oxygen
B. Impurities that must be removed by the liver
C. A high percentage of carbon dioxide
D. All the wastes to be delivered to the organs of excretion
Answer: A. A full supply of oxygen
Rationale: The left ventricle pumps oxygenated (arterial) blood into the aorta for systemic distribution, so it carries a full supply of oxygen. Deoxygenated, CO2-rich blood is handled by the right side of the heart (Anatomy & Physiology of the cardiovascular system).
371. Which problem constitutes a medical emergency
A. Anuria
B. Polyuria
C. Dysuria
D. Dyspnea
Answer: A. Anuria
Rationale: Anuria (absence of urine output, <100 ml/24h) signals acute kidney failure or complete obstruction and constitutes a medical emergency requiring urgent intervention to prevent fatal uraemia and hyperkalaemia. Polyuria, dysuria and dyspnea are significant but the total cessation of urine production is the true renal emergency here. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
372. Which goal would have priority in planning care of the aging patient with urinary incontinence?
A. Recognizes the urge to void
B. Mobility necessary for toileting independently
C. Episodes of incontinence decrease
D. Drinks a minimum of 2000ml of fluid per day
Answer: C. Episodes of incontinence decrease
Rationale: For an aging patient with urinary incontinence, the priority goal is a reduction in incontinence episodes, which is the direct desired outcome addressing the nursing problem. Recognising urge, mobility and adequate fluids are contributing interventions/means, whereas decreased episodes is the overall priority outcome. Reference: Kozier Fundamentals of Nursing; Brunner & Suddarth.
373. Russell’s traction is indicated in;
A. Femur,knee and hip fractures
B. Minor fractures of lower spine,sciatica,muscle spasms
C. Degenerative disc disease of cervical spine,shoulder dislocation,muscle spasm
D. Femur,acetabulum and lower leg fracture
Answer: A. Femur,knee and hip fractures
Rationale: Russell's traction combines skin traction with a knee sling and is used for fractures of the femur, and injuries of the knee and hip. It is not indicated for spinal, cervical or shoulder conditions. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing (orthopaedic traction).
374. Joints usually affected by osteoarthritis are;
A. Thoracic spine,knees,wrist
B. Acromioclavicular,sacral spine,lower cervical spine
C. Ankle,sacro-iliac,hip
D. Thoracic spine, hip,temporal mandibular
Answer: C. Ankle,sacro-iliac,hip
Rationale: Osteoarthritis characteristically affects weight-bearing joints, and of the options provided this set contains the hip (a classic OA joint) and the ankle, both load-bearing (Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing). The other option sets emphasise non-weight-bearing or spinal/inflammatory-pattern joints (acromioclavicular, cervical/thoracic spine, TMJ) that are less typical of primary OA. Option C is the best fit for weight-bearing joint involvement, though all sets are imperfect.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
375. Status asthmaticus can be precipitated by;
A. Infection,tranquilizers overdose, emphysema,anxiety
B. Tranquilizers overdose,nebulizer abuse,anxiety,infection
C. Tranquilizers overdose,anxiety,blebs and aspirin overdose
D. Empysema,infection,nebulizer abuse and bullae
Answer: B. Tranquilizers overdose,nebulizer abuse,anxiety,infection
Rationale: Status asthmaticus is precipitated by respiratory infection, overuse/abuse of nebulizers (rebound), tranquilizer overdose depressing respiration, and anxiety. Emphysema, blebs and bullae are structural COPD findings, not precipitants of status asthmaticus. Reference: Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
376. Broncho-pneumonea presents with,
A. Stabbing chest pain,low grade fever,dry cough and low pulse.
B. Nasal flaring, fever, cyanosis and stabbing chest pain
C. Rapid pulse,nasal flaring, barrel chest and low grade fever
D. Low grade fever,nasal flaring,stabbing chest pain and low pulse.
Answer: B. Nasal flaring, fever, cyanosis and stabbing chest pain
Rationale: Bronchopneumonia presents with fever, nasal flaring, cyanosis (from impaired gas exchange) and pleuritic (stabbing) chest pain along with rapid pulse. The options citing low-grade fever with low pulse do not fit the typical high fever and tachycardia of bronchopneumonia. Reference: Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols; Brunner & Suddarth.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
377. The main role played by the anaesthetic nurse is,
A. Intubating patient.
B. Extubating patient
C. Observing patient
D. Supporting the scrub nurse
Answer: C. Observing patient
Rationale: The anaesthetic nurse's main role is continuous observation and monitoring of the patient's vital signs and condition during anaesthesia, assisting the anaesthetist. Intubation and extubation are performed by the anaesthetist, and the anaesthetic nurse supports the anaesthetist rather than the scrub nurse. Reference: NCK perioperative nursing standards.
378. The following information about a patient is classified as objective data,
A. Feels tired, blood pressure 130/70mmHg,leg hurts
B. Dry skin, nocturia, anorexia
C. Seems nervous, temperature 370 C, pale
D. Allergic to sulphur drugs, skin rash, lethargic
Answer: C. Seems nervous, temperature 370 C, pale
Rationale: Objective data are observable/measurable by the nurse. 'Seems nervous', a measured temperature of 37 C, and observed pallor are all objectively observable, whereas fatigue, pain, nocturia history and stated allergies are subjective (patient-reported). Reference: Kozier Fundamentals of Nursing (nursing process assessment).
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
379. A patient is to receive 500mls of Normal saline over a period of 6 hrs. Using a fluid giving set with a drop factor of 20dpm, the nurse should regulate the flow rate to,
A. 27 drops/minute.
B. 28 drops/ minute.
C. 43 drops/minute.
D. 44 drops/minute
Answer: B. 28 drops/ minute.
Rationale: Flow rate = (volume x drop factor)/time in minutes = (500 x 20)/(6 x 60) = 10000/360 = 27.8, which rounds to 28 drops per minute. Reference: Kozier Fundamentals of Nursing (IV fluid calculation).
380. A deteriorating condition for a patient with head injury will be indicated by,
A. Widening pulse pressure and irregular respiratory pattern.
B. Narrowing pulse pressure and escalation of discomfort.
C. Bradycardia and Kussmaul breathing.
D. Oliguria and narrow pulse pressure
Answer: A. Widening pulse pressure and irregular respiratory pattern.
Rationale: Rising intracranial pressure in head injury produces Cushing's triad: widening pulse pressure (rising systolic with falling diastolic), bradycardia, and an irregular/abnormal respiratory pattern. This signals deterioration and impending herniation. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
381. The substances that are completely reabsorbed by renal tubules after filtration (high- threshold materials) include,
A. Creatinine,urea,uric acid
B. Water,urine, plasma proteins
C. Bicarbonate,glucose,amino-acids
D. Sodium,potassium,phosphate
Answer: C. Bicarbonate,glucose,amino-acids
Rationale: High-threshold substances are almost completely reabsorbed by the renal tubules under normal conditions: glucose, amino acids and bicarbonate. Creatinine and urea are waste products largely excreted, and plasma proteins are not normally filtered. Per Guyton/standard renal physiology as taught in NCK anatomy & physiology.
382. Late symptoms of cervical cancer include,
A. Painful micturition, haematuria
B. Rectal bleeding, referred flank pain
C. Painless bleeding, menorrhagia.
D. Oedema of exteremities, thin watery vaginal discharge
Answer: D. Oedema of exteremities, thin watery vaginal discharge
Rationale: Late (advanced) cervical cancer presents with lower limb oedema from lymphatic/vascular obstruction and a foul, thin watery/blood-stained vaginal discharge as the tumour spreads. Painless bleeding and abnormal discharge are earlier signs, while the advanced-stage picture includes leg oedema. Per Brunner & Suddarth and Kenya cervical cancer screening guidelines.
⚠ Ambiguous — more than one defensible answer
383. The motor abilities observed in a 5 months old infant include,
A. Able to sit when supported, turns over front to back.
B. Able to sit unsupported, cruises easily around furniture.
C. Able to pull self to standing position, transfers objects hand to hand.
D. Can hold to small objects, holds hands tightly closed
Answer: A. Able to sit when supported, turns over front to back.
Rationale: At about 5 months an infant sits with support and rolls over (front to back), holding the head steady. Sitting unsupported, cruising and pulling to stand are later milestones (8-12 months). Per Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols and developmental milestone charts.
384. In non-communicating forms of hydrocephalus,
A. CSF formation is normal but re-absorption is abnormal
B. CSF re-absorption is normal but formation is abnormal
C. The obstruction exists between the choroid plexi and subarachnoid space
D. The obstruction exists between the foramen magnum and the central canal
Answer: C. The obstruction exists between the choroid plexi and subarachnoid space
Rationale: Non-communicating (obstructive) hydrocephalus is caused by a blockage within the ventricular system so that CSF cannot flow from the choroid plexus (site of formation) into the subarachnoid space (Brunner & Suddarth neurology). Communicating hydrocephalus, by contrast, has normal formation with impaired reabsorption. The defining feature is the obstruction located between the choroid plexi and the subarachnoid space.
385. In right sided (diastolic) heart failure,
A. Patients present with distended Jugular vein
B. There is compensatory tachycardia
C. There is production of frothy sputum
D. Patients complain of light headedness
Answer: A. Patients present with distended Jugular vein
Rationale: Right-sided heart failure causes systemic venous congestion, manifesting as raised jugular venous pressure (distended jugular veins), peripheral oedema and hepatomegaly. Frothy sputum and light-headedness are features of left-sided (pulmonary) failure. Per Brunner & Suddarth Medical-Surgical Nursing.
KRCHN — Paper II
Year: 2022
1. The three parameters of steam sterilization include:-
A. Steam under pressure, time, and temperature
B. Time, temperature, and concentration
C. Temperature, time and humidity
D. Steam, personnel, autoclave
Answer: A. Steam under pressure, time, and temperature
Rationale: The critical parameters for effective steam (autoclave) sterilization are steam under pressure, exposure time, and temperature (e.g. 121°C/15 psi/15-30 min or 134°C for shorter cycles). This is per Kenya IPC guidelines and standard sterilization principles.
2. Autoclaving: -
